Alexandre Ghiti

1 items
Patches Submitted (1)
Last comment 2026-10-22 [RFC PATCH 0/1] mm/swap: keep reclaim batches contiguous on swap
Show 1 individual patches
  • [1/1] mm/swap: allocate contiguous swap slots for a reclaim batch
Neutral LLM (per-reviewer)

This patch aims to reduce the number of I/O requests on a swap device by keeping reclaim batches contiguous. The current implementation of swap allocation and deallocation can lead to scattered writes, resulting in increased I/O requests. The patch modifies the allocator to allocate contiguous free slots for a reclaim batch, reducing the number of small requests and improving throughput. This is achieved by informing the allocator about the size of the batch and allowing it to allocate contiguous slots accordingly. The goal is to reduce the overhead of swap I/O operations and improve system performance.

3 reviewer comments — 0 participants
0 participants

Reviewer Kairui Song suggested reverting commit 9a42aed48421, which prefers nonfull over free clusters for small order allocations, and instead always scanning full clusters to prioritize contiguity on large physical swap devices.
Show original comment

That's a interesting problem. I think the real problem is with commit
9a42aed48421 ("mm, swap: prefer nonfull over free clusters"), right?

It's more like a balance between fragmentation and contiguity of small
order allocation issue.

Perhaps we should instead always scan the full clusters and revert
that one commit? That commit was only following the idea of keeping
the allocation more compacted for that series, it looked good at that
time. Since fragmentation issues can be solved in other ways,
contiguity seems to matter more for large physical swap devices.

Boris Burkov

8 items
Patches Submitted (8)
[PATCH v2 0/7] btrfs: allocator perf improvements Neutral llm-chunked-no-replies

This patch series improves the performance of Btrfs's allocator by reducing unnecessary operations on block groups, particularly in large and mostly full file systems. The changes include skipping unhelpful size class relaxing allocation loops, removing redundant checks, and optimizing the find_free_extent function to skip locked block groups and size classes. These improvements result in significant speedups for certain workloads, such as running 20k fallocates on a sparse file system, which was previously taking around 40 seconds but is now completed in about 7 seconds.

[PATCH v2 1/7] btrfs: skip unhelpful size class relaxing allocation loops Neutral llm-chunked-no-replies

This patch optimizes the Btrfs extent tree by skipping unnecessary loops when size class is not a factor. It introduces a new flag to track whether a block group was skipped due to its size class, and only runs loops that are relevant based on this information. This change aims to reduce unnecessary computations and improve performance in certain scenarios.

[PATCH v2 2/7] btrfs: don't take block group references in find_free_extent() Neutral llm-chunked-no-replies

This patch optimizes the find_free_extent() function in Btrfs by removing unnecessary get/put pairs on block group references. The get/put pair was pointless because the loop is already protected by a semaphore, making it impossible for this to be the last reference. This change saves time on filesystems with many full block groups and reduces the overhead of managing block group references.

[PATCH v2 3/7] btrfs: remove redundant checks in find_free_extent() hint path Neutral llm-chunked-no-replies

This patch removes redundant checks in the find_free_extent() function's hint path, making it more efficient and reducing unnecessary complexity. The hint is a long-lasting reference to a block group that can outlast its destruction or be destroyed while still referenced. The patch simplifies the check for hints by only verifying their attachment to the list of block groups, allowing shared checks with normally reached block groups. This change improves code organization and reduces potential bugs caused by redundant checks.

[PATCH v2 4/7] btrfs: factor the find_free_extent() block group skip into a helper Neutral llm-chunked-no-replies

This patch refactors a complex condition in the find_free_extent() function into a separate helper function called find_free_extent_skip_block_group(). This makes the code easier to understand and maintain by breaking down a large, unwieldy block of logic into smaller, more manageable pieces. The new function checks if a block group can be skipped due to read-only status or other conditions, and returns true if it should be skipped. No functional changes are made.

[PATCH v2 5/7] btrfs: find_free_extent checks full block groups unlocked Neutral llm-chunked-no-replies

This patch optimizes the btrfs find_free_extent function by skipping block groups that are full and cannot satisfy an allocation request, without taking a lock. This is done because checking if a block group can satisfy an allocation does not guarantee that it will be able to do so when locked later. The patch also includes performance measurements showing significant improvements in certain scenarios.

[PATCH v2 6/7] btrfs: find_free_extent checks size class unlocked Neutral llm-chunked-no-replies

This patch modifies the find_free_extent function in Btrfs to skip block groups that have an incorrect size class without acquiring a lock on the block group. This is done to improve performance by reducing the number of locks taken, especially in large file systems where thousands of block groups may need to be checked. The patch also includes benchmarking results showing improved performance after applying this change.

[PATCH v2 7/7] btrfs: remove block_group data_rwsem and delalloc_bytes Neutral llm-chunked-no-replies

This patch removes the `data_rwsem` and `delalloc_bytes` fields from the Btrfs block group data structure, as they are no longer needed after the removal of the v1 space cache in a previous commit. The `data_rwsem` was only used by the v1 space cache write path, which has been removed, and `delalloc_bytes` is now only printed but not used. Removing these fields simplifies the code and eliminates confusing indirection machinery dedicated to separating delalloc calls from non-delalloc calls.

Gregory Price

Patches Submitted (14)
Last comment 2026-10-23 [PATCH] cgroup/cpuset: check effective mems for a movable-only configuration Neutral LLM (per-reviewer)

This patch modifies the cgroup/cpuset code to check for effective memory settings in a movable-only configuration. The change moves the check from the hotplug path to the update_nodemasks_hier function, where it can be performed more accurately. This prevents warnings when a task requests a movable node that is not present in its parent's nodemask, but will still warn if the parent's mems include the node later. The patch also removes an unnecessary check in the update_nodemask function.

Last comment 2026-10-19 [PATCH v2] cgroup/cpuset: use mems_allowed_seq when checking a policy nodemask Neutral LLM

This patch addresses a potential performance issue in the cgroup/cpuset subsystem by introducing a retry mechanism when checking a policy nodemask against the current task's mems_allowed. The problem arises from the fact that cpuset updates rebind the task's mempolicy and change mems_allowed in separate steps, which can cause a spurious numa placement miss under certain conditions. The patch uses the mems_allowed_seq to retry the intersection until it is stable.

Ongoing Submitted 2026-10-16 Last comment 2026-10-21 ⏱ No recent activity [PATCH 0/6] daxctl: use dax kmem atomic whole-device hotplug 6 patches
Show 6 individual patches
  • [1/6] libdaxctl: add dax kmem atomic hotplug state accessors Neutral Submitted 2026-10-16Last comment 2026-10-21 Alison Schofield
  • [2/6] daxctl: online whole-device memory atomically when available Neutral Submitted 2026-10-16Last comment 2026-10-21 Alison Schofield
  • [3/6] daxctl: offline whole-device memory via atomic unplug when available Neutral Submitted 2026-10-16Last comment 2026-10-21 Alison Schofield
  • [4/6] daxctl: report whole-device hotplug state in 'daxctl list' Neutral Submitted 2026-10-16Last comment 2026-10-21 Alison Schofield
  • [5/6] Documentation: describe atomic whole-device hotplug in daxctl man pages Neutral Submitted 2026-10-16Last comment 2026-10-21 Alison Schofield
  • [6/6] test/daxctl-devices.sh: cycle offline/online to cover unplug and replug Neutral Submitted 2026-10-16Last comment 2026-10-21 Alison Schofield
Neutral LLM (per-reviewer)

This patch series introduces support for atomic whole-device hotplug in daxctl using the dax_kmem attribute. This allows for safe and concurrent online and offline operations on a device, preventing races between agents like udev and auto-online. The patch also updates the 'daxctl list' command to report the hotplug state of devices, which can be inconsistent if blocks are toggled individually. The changes maintain compatibility with older daxctl versions and handle cases where unplug or re-adding fails due to lack of memory hotremove support or region reservation issues.

Alison Schofield Inline Review Reviewed-by Needs Work LLM
Reviewer noted that using DAXCTL_KMEM_ONLINE in the atomic --no-movable path could place memory in ZONE_MOVABLE despite the '--no-movable' flag, and suggested either switching to DAXCTL_KMEM_ONLINE_KERNEL or adding a zone check similar to the per-block path. Reviewer noted that a 'reconfigure-device -m system-ram' command may add memory twice, first offline by kmem at probe time and then online by daxctl after unplugging it, potentially leaving the device unplugged if re-add fails. Alison requested the kernel interface support transitioning directly from an offline to an online state instead of falling back to per-block onlining.
Show original comment 
Hi Greg, 

A couple of things here...

The atomic --no-movable path uses DAXCTL_KMEM_ONLINE, leaving zone selection
to the kernel. With memory_hotplug.online_policy=auto-movable, IIUC this could
place memory in ZONE_MOVABLE despite --no-movable.

The per-block path checks the resulting zone and fails if it isn't ZONE_NORMAL,
but the atomic path skips that check.

Should this use DAXCTL_KMEM_ONLINE_KERNEL instead, or retain DAXCTL_KMEM_ONLINE
and add the same zone check?

---

With this, a "reconfigure-device -m system-ram" may add the memory twice.
First kmem adds it offline at probe and then daxctl unplugs it and adds it
back online. If that re-add fails, the device is left unplugged rather than
offline.

I get that falling back to per-block onlining would reopen the race this
series is addressing. Could the kernel interface support transitioning
directly from offline to an online state instead? It seems that would avoid
both the extra hotplug work and the left-unplugged failure case.

-- Alison

Hi Greg,

I see you called this out in the cover letter, and that the kernel is
deliberately combining offlining and removing memory to avoid the race
before unbind.

But, this does change the behavior of the command "daxctl offline-memory".
Previously it left memory blocks present but offline and now it removes

offline, and use atomic unplug only when reconfiguring the device?

---

When num_on == 0, another agent could online a block between the earlier
check and the unplug and that memory would then be removed.

I see this noted in the commit message. Is that worth documenting for
users?


-- Alison

Alison Schofield Reviewed-by Needs Work LLM
Reviewer Alison Schofield requested that tests handle the absence of a new sysfs attribute by failing loudly when the functionality is missing, and suggested adding state-dependent test cases to cover kernel selftest limitations.
Show original comment 
Hi Greg,

Please change how the tests handle the absence of the new sysfs attribute.
If a test expects the new interface, a missing attribute should cause
the test to fail, with a clear error message. We have had problems in
the past with tests silently not executing letting defects go undetected.

Note that we don't require backward compatibility for the unit tests.
The utilities must continue working with older kernels, but tests for new
functionality should fail loudly when that functionality is missing.
(I'm aware that not all tests follow that guideline, and they need fixing.)

Please also leave the existing test cases as they were and run the new
state-dependent cases last.

A few stale state cases would exercise daxctl behavior that the kernel
selftest can't cover - 

  - Offline one memory block out-of-band, verify that daxctl list
    reports hotplug_state "inconsistent", then verify that
    offline-memory still leaves the device unplugged with its blocks
    removed.

  - Offline all blocks out-of-band, then verify that
    "reconfigure-device -m devdax" succeeds and the blocks are removed.

  - With all blocks offline but the device state still reporting
    online, verify that online-memory falls back to the per-block path
    and brings everything online.

For "blocks removed", please check that the device's memory blocks
are gone from /sys/devices/system/memory.  ie verify in sysfs directly
independent of the libdaxctl code being tested.

About multi-range devices-

I found that libdaxctl undercounts memory blocks on multi-range devices.
I'm posting a separate fix and test for that. Nothing in this series needs
to change. I'll cc you for review.

Our ndctl emulation can't exercise multi-range hotplug, but the kernel
selftest can run against a real multi-range device. Could you add coverage
there, including the partial-add case where the kernel reports success
even though not all ranges were added?

-- Alison

Ongoing Submitted 2026-10-14 ⏱ No comments yet [PATCH] fixup! selftests/mm: check MADV_COLD and MADV_PAGEOUT range handling Neutral llm-chunked-no-replies

This patch fixes a bug in the selftests/mm/madvise.c file where sched_getcpu() can return a CPU number above the maximum allowed, causing sched_setaffinity() to fail. The fix is to dynamically allocate memory for the cpu_set_t mask using CPU_ALLOC(), rather than relying on a fixed-size mask.

Last comment 2026-10-19 [PATCH] mm/memory-tiers: reset demotion targets of nodes without memory Neutral LLM

This patch resets the demotion targets of nodes without memory to prevent stale entries from being used by reclaimers. The current implementation only resets nodes that have N_MEMORY set, which can lead to incorrect demotion targets when a node regains its last memory block and rebuilds its targets.

Last comment 2026-10-20 [PATCH v2] mm/memory-tiers: reset demotion targets of nodes without memory Neutral LLM

The patch addresses a bug in the memory-tiers code, where demotion targets of nodes without memory are not properly reset. This causes stale entries to remain in node_demotion[nid].preferred, which can lead to incorrect behavior when a node regains memory and its targets are rebuilt. The patch resets the targets of every node, ensuring that only valid targets are used.

1 reviewer comment — 0 participants
0 participants

Identified the issue as a minor bug that doesn't have any user-visible effects. Approved the patch and added a Reviewed-by tag.

>

> and its targets being rebuilt, and it filters the entry against the
> current allowed targets, so there is no user-visible effect. To see it,
> online and then offline a node's only DIMM: a debugger still finds the
> lower-tier node in node_demotion[nid].preferred.
>
> Reset the targets of every node.
>
> Fixes: 6c542ab75714 ("mm/demotion: build demotion targets based on explicit memory tiers")
> Signed-off-by: Gregory Price (Meta) <gourry@gourry.net>

Ongoing Submitted 2026-10-15 Last comment 2026-10-19 ⏱ No recent activity [PATCH] mm/mempolicy: convert mempolicy refcnt to refcount_t Neutral LLM

This patch converts the reference counting mechanism in the memory policy (mempolicy) from atomic_t to refcount_t, which provides overflow and underflow protection. This change silences a static analysis warning related to __mpol_put() and kmem_cache_free(). The patch does not introduce any functional changes for correct callers.

[PATCH] mm/mempolicy: recompute automatic interleave weights on memory hotplug Neutral llm-chunked-no-replies

This patch updates the automatic interleave weights in the Linux kernel's memory policy module to recalculate weights based on node bandwidth when a node gains or loses memory. The previous behavior was to only update weights when a new node reports its bandwidth, leading to incorrect weights being used. The patch introduces a new function `wi_state_refresh` that recalculates the weights from the recorded bandwidth whenever a node gains its first memory or loses its last. This ensures that the weighted interleave mode works correctly even after nodes are added or removed.

Last comment 2026-10-19 [PATCH] mm/page_alloc: let the highest memory node recover from hopeless state Neutral LLM (per-reviewer)

This patch fixes a problem where the highest memory node in a system cannot recover from a 'hopeless' state, even when its zone recovers above the high watermark. The issue arises because the current code checks if there is another memory node available for allocations to fall back to by looking at the next higher-numbered node ID, which always returns false for the highest node. This patch changes the check to count the number of nodes with a state of N_MEMORY, effectively checking if any other node exists. As a result, the highest node can now recover from its hopeless state and kswapd will be re-enabled.

[PATCH v2 0/5] mm: fix allocator races with memory hotplug 5 patches
Show 5 individual patches
  • [1/5] mm/page_alloc: detect zonelist rebuilds from memory hot-add
  • [2/5] mm/page_alloc: validate zonelist slots against concurrent rebuilds
  • [3/5] mm/page_alloc: snapshot the preferred zone against shrink rebuilds
  • [4/5] mm/page_alloc: keep a zone's pagesets when it goes empty
  • [5/5] mm/mmzone: read each zoneref slot once when filtering by nodemask
Neutral llm-chunked-no-replies

This patch series addresses five races in the Linux kernel's memory allocator that can occur when memory hotplug is used to add or remove physical memory from a system. The issues arise due to concurrent access and updates of zone lists, page sets, and other data structures by multiple CPUs. The patches fix these problems by introducing retries for certain operations, ensuring proper synchronization, and preventing dereferences of NULL pointers. The approach taken involves modifying various kernel functions and data structures to handle the hotplug events correctly and safely.

Last comment 2026-10-21 [PATCH v3 0/8] mm: fix allocator races with memory hotplug 8 patches
Show 8 individual patches
  • [1/8] mm/page_alloc: detect zonelist rebuilds from memory hot-add
  • [2/8] mm/page_alloc: snapshot the preferred zone against shrink rebuilds
  • [3/8] mm/mmzone: read each zoneref slot once when filtering by nodemask
  • [4/8] mm/page_alloc: pass the zone, not the zoneref, to reclaim_retry_zone
  • [5/8] mm/page_alloc: retry allocations that raced with a zonelist rebuild
  • [6/8] mm/hugetlb: retry the pool walk when the zonelist is rebuilt
  • [7/8] mm/page_alloc: retry alloc_contig_pages() when the zonelist is rebuilt
  • [8/8] mm/page_alloc: keep a zone's pagesets when it goes empty
Neutral LLM (per-reviewer)

This patch series addresses eight race conditions between memory hotplug and the Linux allocator, which can lead to failed allocations, NULL dereferences, or incorrect placement of pages. The races occur because zonelists are reordered in-place at runtime during hotplug, causing narrow but determinable windows for failure. The patches use a kprobe reproduction module to make these conditions deterministic and fix the issues by synchronizing access to zonelists and preferred zones.

Ongoing Submitted 2026-10-14 Last comment 2026-10-19 ⏱ No recent activity [PATCH 0/2] mm: fix two allocator races with zonelist and cpuset updates 2 patches
Show 2 individual patches
  • [1/2] mm/page_alloc: detect zonelist rebuilds from memory hot-add Neutral Submitted 2026-10-14 Vlastimil Babka (SUSE)
  • [2/2] cgroup/cpuset: use mems_allowed_seq when checking a policy nodemask Neutral Submitted 2026-10-14Last comment 2026-10-19 Gregory Price (author) Vlastimil (SUSE) Waiman Long
Neutral LLM (per-reviewer)

This patch addresses two races in the Linux kernel's memory allocator that can occur when updating zonelists and cpusets concurrently. The first issue arises from a retry mechanism for zonelist updates, which can lead to inconsistent views of state between CPUs. The second problem involves a comparison between a mempolicy nodemask and the current mems_allowed without considering the sequence number, resulting in spurious empty intersections and unnecessary policy relaxations. The patches fix these issues by introducing additional checks and using the correct sequence numbers for updates. These changes aim to prevent allocation failures and ensure that memory policies are correctly enforced.

[PATCH v3 0/7] selftests/mm: add MADV_COLD and MADV_PAGEOUT contract tests 7 patches
Show 7 individual patches
  • [1/7] selftests/mm: add MADV_COLD and MADV_PAGEOUT contract tests
  • [2/7] selftests/mm: check MADV_COLD and MADV_PAGEOUT range handling
  • [3/7] selftests/mm: check that MADV_COLD and MADV_PAGEOUT reject hugetlb
  • [4/7] selftests/mm: check that MADV_COLD and MADV_PAGEOUT preserve shared memory
  • [5/7] selftests/mm: check MADV_COLD and MADV_PAGEOUT on partly advised THPs
  • [6/7] selftests/mm: check MADV_PAGEOUT file permissions
  • [7/7] selftests/mm: check that unauthorized MADV_PAGEOUT skips large file folios
Neutral llm-chunked-no-replies

This patch series adds selftests to cover the contractual behaviors of MADV_COLD and MADV_PAGEOUT, which share a page-table walker in mm/madvise.c. The tests are designed to ensure that userspace can rely on these hints across changes to the walker's implementation. The patches add 24 tests that cover various scenarios, including faulting swapped pages back in, accepting locked VMAs, preserving shared memory, and paging out THPs. The tests also check whether MADV_PAGEOUT can swap/evict pages at all and skip relevant tests if it cannot. The patch series has been tested on a virtme-ng guest with 4 CPUs, two NUMA nodes, a 2G swap disk, and various configurations.

Re: [RFC 0/2] mm: add source selectors to memory.reclaim Neutral llm-chunked-no-replies
Last comment 2026-10-22 Re: [RFC 0/2] mm: add source selectors to memory.reclaim Neutral LLM (per-reviewer)

This patch proposes adding source selectors to memory.reclaim, which would allow for more granular control over memory reclaim policies. The goal is to enable per-tier resident ratio policies, where the system can prioritize reclaim from specific nodes or tiers based on their temperature (coldness). However, some concerns have been raised about the implementation approach, with suggestions that a more fundamental solution, such as tiered memcg counters, would be more effective and less prone to workarounds.

2 reviewer comments — 0 participants
0 participants
Li Zhe ↳ replying to Gregory (Meta) Reviewed-by Needs Work LLM
Li Zhe expressed concerns that the patch's approach using source selectors is not a replacement for implementing tiered memcg counters/limits, which would provide accounting and policy targets. He suggested that these directions are compatible and can be used together to improve kernel-driven behavior and user-space reclaim pressure. Li Zhe noted that the proposed memory.reclaim algorithm does not address the underlying issues of reclaim, such as inverting the LRU and applying per-tier memcg limits, and instead aims to expose a source-scoped proactive reclaim actuator while keeping the kernel's reclaim policy in control. He expressed concern that this approach may be a 'hack' to avoid addressing these harder problems. Reviewer Li Zhe pointed out that using `source=nodes` in memory.reclaim does not guarantee a one-shot net footprint change and instead requires a feedback loop of applying reclaim pressure, observing the new distribution, and iterating. They also disagreed with constraining zswap pool allocations based on source nodes. Reviewer Li Zhe expressed concerns that the patch's reclaim interface may be a 'hack' to avoid addressing more fundamental issues in memory management, such as per-tier memcg counters and correct LRU handling. He suggested adding details to the cover letter demonstrating how per-node resident ratios work and requested clarification on what constitutes progress under different scenarios.
Show original comment 
Thanks for the detailed comments.

I agree that improving the default proactive/async reclaim behavior can
be useful, and I also agree that tiered memcg counters/limits would be
useful. I do not see source=nodes as a replacement for those directions.
They solve different parts of the problem: tiered counters/limits provide
accounting and policy targets, while source=nodes is only an actuator for
proactive reclaim.

So I think these directions are compatible. A better default reclaim
policy or tiered memcg limits can improve kernel-driven behavior, while
source=nodes gives userspace an explicit way to apply reclaim pressure
to a selected resident source.

---

That is fair, and I do not intend this to avoid improving reclaim or
adding better tier-aware memcg accounting. A better memory.reclaim
algorithm would also make source=nodes more useful, because source=nodes
still uses the existing reclaim path after selecting the resident source.
The goal here is narrower: expose a source-scoped proactive reclaim
actuator while keeping the kernel's reclaim policy in control. The old
nodes= discussion had ABI problems, but it also showed that a
nodemask-like source selector is a useful primitive for finer-grained
proactive reclaim control [1].

---

This is a good example of why source=nodes should not be described as a
one-shot net footprint guarantee. Assuming source=nodes:1, the request
applies reclaim pressure to memcg memory resident on node1. That may
free some space on node1 in the short term, which can provide room for a
later node0-to-node1 demotion step. If zswap allocation, kswapd, or memcg
reclaim later moves pressure back across tiers and node1 usage rises
again, that is part of the dynamic reclaim/tiering behavior.

So userspace cannot rely on one write to memory.reclaim to produce a
final per-node distribution. The intended usage is a feedback loop: the
controller observes the memcg's resident distribution, chooses the source
nodes it wants to relieve, applies source-scoped reclaim pressure,
observes the new distribution, and iterates.

I do not think this patch should make source=nodes constrain zswap pool
allocations. That would be a different and much stronger semantic. The
current intent is only to constrain the resident source from which memcg
reclaim applies pressure, while completion accounting remains the
existing memory.reclaim accounting.

---

Agreed. I will update the cover letter to make this model clearer. I
will also make clear that source=nodes is not a final net per-node
footprint guarantee and does not change memory.reclaim completion
accounting.

[1] https://lkml.org/lkml/2022/12/14/194

Thanks,
Zhe

[PATCH nfs-utils v2 0/7] mountd/exportfs/nfsref: netlink loose ends and bug fixes 7 patches

Neutral llm-chunked-no-replies

This patch series addresses issues in the NFS-utils code, specifically in mountd and exportfs, where it was using /proc instead of netlink when available. The patches fix these bugs, clean up code, and add a MAINTAINERS file to improve maintainability. They also introduce changes to handle path-based UUIDs for pseudo-root directories and update the get_maintainer.pl script.

[PATCH nfs-utils] mountd: fetch root filehandles over netlink Neutral llm-chunked-no-replies

This patch modifies the mountd service in nfs-utils to fetch root filehandles over netlink instead of relying on /proc/fs/nfsd/filehandle. It introduces a new command, NFSD_CMD_FILEHANDLE_GET, which is used first to retrieve the filehandle from the kernel's cache. If this fails or if the kernel does not support the new command, it falls back to using /proc/fs/nfsd/filehandle as before. The patch also updates the configure probe to require the new command and attributes in nfsd_netlink.h.

[PATCH nfs-utils v2] mountd: fetch root filehandles over netlink Neutral llm-chunked-no-replies

This patch optimizes the mountd service by fetching root filehandles over netlink, a kernel-based communication protocol. When netlink is unavailable or the kernel predates the new command, it falls back to using /proc/fs/nfsd/filehandle. The patch also requires the use of the new NFSD_CMD_FILEHANDLE_GET command and its associated attributes in the configure probe. This change aims to improve performance by reducing the number of failed attempts when netlink is not available.

Last comment 2026-10-20 [PATCH] nfsd: add NFSD_CMD_FILEHANDLE_GET for root filehandle lookup Neutral LLM (per-reviewer)

This patch adds a new netlink command, NFSD_CMD_FILEHANDLE_GET, to the NFS server (nfsd) that allows clients to look up the root filehandle of an export for a specific client domain. The command takes three parameters: the client domain name, the path of the export, and the maximum size of the filehandle that can be accepted by the client. The patch also includes a kselftest to validate the new command's behavior and ensure it matches the existing /proc/fs/nfsd/filehandle transaction interface used by rpc.mountd. A companion patch for nfs-utils will be forthcoming.

Chuck Lever ↳ replying to Jeff Layton Inline Review Reviewed-by Needs Work LLM
Reviewer noted four technical issues: the selftest's proc_filehandle() function fails to parse the /proc reply due to a prefixing issue, the new binary is missing from .gitignore, the code silently clamps handle size to NFS3_FHSIZE instead of capping it at maxsize as claimed in the yaml doc and kernel-doc, and fh_proc_mount() leaves self->mnt behind when mount(2) fails after mkdir() succeeds.
Show original comment 
Hi Jeff, thanks for the patch. Review findings:

1. The selftest's proc_filehandle() cannot parse the /proc reply.
   write_filehandle() emits the handle via qword_addhex(), which
   prefixes the hex digits with "\x" (net/sunrpc/cache.c), so the
   first hexval() call fails and the function returns -EIO.

2. The new binary is missing from tools/testing/selftests/nfsd/
   .gitignore, unlike the four sibling programs listed there.

3. The yaml doc for maxsize and the kernel-doc claim the handle is
   capped at @maxsize, but the code silently clamps to NFS3_FHSIZE.
   A caller passing 128 gets a 64-byte handle with no error.

4. My sashiko instance here noted that fh_proc_mount() leaves
   self->mnt behind when mount(2) fails after mkdir() succeeds, so
   teardown's rmdir() of the tmpdir fails.

Can you send a v2 that addresses these issues?


-- 
Chuck Lever (Come to NFS bake-a-thon! https://nfsv4bat.org)

Mike Snitzer ↳ replying to Jeff Layton Reviewed-by Needs Work LLM
The reviewer, Mike Snitzer, reviewed the sashiko review of the patch and found three issues that needed to be addressed in v9. Show original comment 
Thanks, I've now trained my claude monkey with it. But I'm sure it'll
still throw feces periodically.

Also, I've reviewed the sashiko review of this v8, that you kindly
forwarded this morning, and 3 of the 7 issues it flagged were real.

I don't want to spam the lists more than I already have by immediately
posting v9... but it has needed changes, blech.  I'll at least wait to
see if Chuck picks up 1/7 to fold into the commit it fixes which is
already staged in cel/nfsd-testing (and if so I'll move Chuck and Jeff
to cc and upgrade -mm and -fsdevel to the To: for v9's posting...)

But here is the changelog for pending v9, in case it helps reviewers
side-step reporting something in v8 that is already addressed in v9:

Changes since v8, from the sashiko findings Jeff Layton relayed:
- 2/7 and 4/7: a folio is counted in WB_DONTCACHE_DIRTY only when both
  PG_dirty and its dirty tag are set.  v8 tested the tag alone.
  __folio_cancel_dirty() clears PG_dirty but leaves the tag, so a
  cancelled folio that stays cached kept a stale tag.  2/7 then
  uncounted such a folio twice, and 4/7 counted it and never uncounted
  it.
- 7/7: NFSD finds the edges a buffered segment shares by the mapping's
  smallest folio, as iomap does, not by page.  On 16 KiB blocks, a WRITE
  written as one buffered segment can have page-aligned ends inside a
  block.  Nothing completed those edges, and the folios stayed cached
  until reclaim.  NFSD also completes a segment's first edge only when
  the write reached the end of the segment's bytes in that unit.  A
  short write that stopped inside the unit had not written its part.
- 1/7 and 5/7: a paragraph each on a limit the review raised.  1/7
  cannot keep a larger cached folio, left by readahead, from spanning
  the cut.  5/7 marks the folio before iomap validates the iomap.  A
  stale iomap stopped by a fatal signal then leaves a mark on a folio
  this write did not complete.  Both cost one read-back and no data.
- 3/7 and 6/7: unchanged.

Longer form for the fixes that fell out from the v8 sashiko review:

2/7, the stale dirty tag: real.  __folio_cancel_dirty() uncounts a
dropbehind folio and clears PG_dirty, but leaves PAGECACHE_TAG_DIRTY
set.  Only writeback and truncation clear the tag, and writeback skips
a clean folio.  So a cancelled folio that stays cached keeps a stale
tag, and my clear path uncounted it a second time.  The same stale tag
made folio_mark_dropbehind() in 4/7 count a clean folio that nothing
would uncount.  v9 tests PG_dirty as well as the tag in both places.
A folio is counted only when both are set.  The race the tag closes
stays closed.  A dirtier that has set PG_dirty but not yet counted the
folio has not set the tag either.

7/7, the PAGE_SIZE edge tests: real.  On 16 KiB blocks, a WRITE written
as one buffered segment can have page-aligned ends inside a block.
iomap left those edges for NFSD to complete, and NFSD never asked about
them.  Those folios stayed cached until reclaim.  v9 finds the edges by the
mapping's smallest folio, as iomap does.  Measured on the loop device,
whose dma_alignment of 511 makes every TCP payload memory-misaligned.
NFSD then writes each WRITE as one buffered segment.  32 writers of
12288-byte records: v8 left all 32,736 pages of a 128 MiB file
resident.  v9 leaves 1,348 of 262,080 pages of a 1 GiB file, 0.5%.

7/7, the short write that completes the first edge: real, and the cost
is one read-back on the retry.  v9 completes the first edge only when
the write reached the end of the segment's bytes in that unit.

Patches Submitted (6)
Last comment 2026-10-20 [PATCH v1] memcg: fix cacheline contention between lruvec stat updates and LRU lists Neutral LLM (per-reviewer)

This patch addresses cacheline contention between lruvec stat updates and LRU lists by modifying the update paths to avoid accessing struct mem_cgroup_per_node directly. Instead, it uses the memcg to access the per-cpu lruvec stats, reducing unnecessary cache line fetches and contention on the lru_lock critical section. The patch was motivated by analysis of perf c2c output showing high contention on the first 64 bytes of mem_cgroup_per_node, even though there were no writes to it. The fix is expected to improve performance in multi-threaded workloads with many threads faulting in pages in a single memcg.

Last comment 2026-10-22 [PATCH v2 0/2] memcg: reduce cacheline contention from lruvec stat 2 patches

Neutral LLM

The patch series aims to reduce cacheline contention in the memcg (memory cgroup) lruvec stat updates by rearranging struct mem_cgroup_per_node and optimizing the folio and kmem stat updates. The goal is to improve performance on CPUs with spatial prefetching, such as Intel CPUs.

1 reviewer comment — 0 participants
0 participants
Shakeel Butt Acked-by Positive LLM
Provided Acked-by for both patches and suggested adding a comment to explain why the block was placed in a particular location, mainly due to spatial prefetching on Intel CPUs.
Show original comment 
On Thu, Oct 22, 2026 at 03:39:29PM -0700, Joanne Koong wrote:
> Every memcg lruvec (per-node) stat update reads the memcg and
> lruvec_stats_percpu pointers in the first 64 bytes of struct
> mem_cgroup_per_node. These updates are extremely frequent (eg these
> updates happen several times for every page that's faulted in, freed,
> dirtied, or written back).
> 
> The memcg_pn_read_mostly cacheline group keeps those read-mostly fields
> on a different cacheline from the lruvec, but Intel CPUs do spatial
> prefetching [1] and fetch cachelines in 128-byte aligned pairs.
> Depending on where struct mem_cgroup_per_node lands in its slab, that
> cacheline can be paired with the cacheline holding the LRU list heads.
> 
> When this happens, every stat update also pulls in a copy of the LRU
> list heads cacheline, which is written to constantly by other CPUs under
> the lru_lock. The stat update has to fetch it from another CPU, which is
> unnecessary overhead since the stat update doesn't use the LRU list
> heads at all. But even worse, the lru_lock holder then has to invalidate
> those copies and wait for the acknowledgements to come back before it
> can do its next write, which makes the lru_lock critical section longer
> for everyone waiting on the lock.
> 
> This was found when analyzing the output of perf c2c for a benchmark
> where many threads fault in pages in one memcg. It showed the first 64
> bytes of mem_cgroup_per_node as the most contended cacheline (over a
> third of all HITMs and 5x more than the lru_lock cacheline) even though
> there are no writes to it. A quick experiment adding extra padding so
> that the cacheline no longer shared a pairing with the LRU list heads
> showed that this removed most of the contention, which confirmed the
> cause.
> 
> Fix this by moving the memcg_pn_cold group, which is only touched when
> the memcg is reparented or freed, right after memcg_pn_read_mostly, so
> that the read-mostly cacheline is paired with the cold cacheline
> instead of the LRU list heads.
> 
> Results from a multi-threaded fault/free benchmark in a single memcg,
> run on an Intel Skylake machine (80 threads, 2 NUMA nodes) with threads
> pinned to CPUs showed average pages faulted in (and freed) per second
> across all threads, over 7 freshly created memcgs per run:
> 
>                      before    after
>   shmem, 20 threads  4.74M     5.59M   (+18%)
>   shmem, 80 threads  7.94M     8.66M   (+9%)
>   anon,  20 threads  3.04M     3.08M   (+1%)
> 
> [1] https://www.intel.com/content/dam/doc/manual/64-ia-32-architectures-optimization-manual.pdf
> (bottom of page 62/800)
> 
> Link: https://lore.kernel.org/linux-fsdevel/CAJnrk1ZezmmzrYdf8KP6re_a76izMS=qGPeC8ZZXiteHyZZC-Q@mail.gmail.com/
> Suggested-by: Shakeel Butt <shakeel.butt@linux.dev>
> Signed-off-by: Joanne Koong <joannelkoong@gmail.com>
> ---

>  1 file changed, 11 insertions(+), 11 deletions(-)

Neutral LLM

This patch series aims to reduce cacheline contention between lruvec stat updates and LRU lists by rearranging the struct mem_cgroup_per_node layout and modifying the folio and kmem lruvec stat updates. The goal is to improve performance on CPUs with spatial prefetching, such as Intel CPUs.

Andrew Morton ↳ replying to Joanne Koong Neutral LLM
Mentioned a limitation of the current slab layout that may affect the measured performance improvement. Noted it's not a correctness issue, but rather a potential optimization opportunity.
Show original comment 
On Fri, 23 Oct 2026 12:54:15 -0700 Joanne Koong <joannelkoong@gmail.com> wrote:

> Memcg lruvec stat updates read fields of struct mem_cgroup_per_node and struct
> lruvec that can share a 128-byte cacheline pair with lines the lru_lock holder
> writes constantly (the LRU list heads, lru_lock or lru_zone_size[]), depending
> on where struct mem_cgroup_per_node lands in its slab. On CPUs that do spatial
> prefetching (eg Intel CPUs), this makes the stat updates contend with the
> lru_lock holder.
> 
> Patch 1 rearranges struct mem_cgroup_per_node so that its read-mostly line is
> paired with a cold line, and patch 2 makes the folio and kmem stat updates use
> the memcg and node they already know instead of going through the lruvec,
> whose pgdat field shares a cacheline pair with lru_lock or lru_zone_size[].
> 
> Results on a 2-socket Skylake (80 CPUs), average pages per second for
> threads faulting in and freeing memory in a single memcg:
> 
> 	   base     patch 1   patch 1+2 
> shmem, 20 threads  4.74M    5.59M     5.77M
> shmem, 80 threads  7.94M    8.66M     8.82M
> anon,  20 threads  3.04M    3.08M     3.10M

Thanks.  A local AI review pointed out a thing:

  One limitation worth mentioning in the [1/2] changelog: the slab
  uses SLAB_HWCACHE_ALIGN, which need not align each object to a
  128-byte boundary.  If an object starts 64 bytes into such a pair,
  its first line can still be paired with the end of the preceding
  object rather than its own cold line.  This isn't a correctness
  issue, and the measured Skylake improvement may well be real, but the
  layout doesn't guarantee that every read-mostly line pairs with a
  cold line.
Signals: WAITING_FOR_REVIEW
Last comment 2026-10-22 [PATCH v1] mm: move LRU size stat updates out of lru_lock in folio batches Neutral LLM (per-reviewer)

This patch moves the updates to LRU size statistics out of the lru_lock critical section in folio batches, reducing contention and improving performance. The stats are accumulated while holding the lock and applied after dropping it, resulting in at most one update per LRU list per batch. This change is expected to improve throughput by reducing the time spent waiting for the lru_lock and making the code more efficient.

[PATCH v3 0/3] mm: split underused anonymous mTHP folios 3 patches
Show 3 individual patches

Neutral llm-chunked-no-replies

This patch series addresses memory regressions caused by 2M THP=always on arm64 by allowing the underused shrinker to handle anonymous mTHP folios. The patches modify the thp_underused() function to be size-aware, prevent unnecessary queuing of folios for splitting when underused shrinking is inactive, and add anonymous mTHP folios to the deferred split list with a minimum size of 2M.

Last comment 2026-10-20 [PATCH v3] mm: unqueue deferred split folios outside the lruvec lock Neutral LLM (per-reviewer)

This patch modifies the Linux kernel to unqueue deferred split folios outside of the lruvec lock, which is currently held in various paths such as move_folios_to_lru() and folio_batch_move_lru(). The lruvec lock protects the folio's LRU list and reclaim lists, but the deferred split queue lives on a separate list_lru with its own per-memcg per-node lock. By unqueuing the folios outside of the lruvec lock, the patch reduces contention on this critical section, which is highly contended and runs with IRQs disabled. This change was motivated by performance issues observed in a fault microbenchmark, where queueing small mTHP folios on the deferred split queue caused significant lock contention.

Last comment 2026-10-20 [PATCH] mm/memcontrol: publish null pointer before dropping css reference Neutral LLM (per-reviewer)

This patch fixes a rare issue where a memory cgroup (memcg) could be freed while still being referenced by the memcg's stock, leading to a null pointer dereference. The fix involves publishing a NULL pointer before dropping the reference to the cgroup, ensuring that any readers waiting for the RCU grace period are properly notified and can safely access the now-freed memory. This change is based on a report from Sashiko and fixes an issue introduced in a previous patch series by Joshua Hahn.

5 reviewer comments — 0 participants
0 participants

Reviewer Shakeel Butt noted that the css_put call in drain_stock cannot be the last reference for the memcg because the caller of consume_stock is holding a reference to the memcg, and requested the patch to address this issue.

Shakeel Butt ↳ replying to Joshua Hahn Inline Review Reviewed-by Needs Work LLM
Reviewer Shakeel Butt questioned the existence of a race condition in the patch, citing a scenario where CPU 1 and CPU 2 can access the same memcg simultaneously due to PREEMPT_RT's behavior with per-CPU spinlocks. He used AI reasoning to conclude that this is indeed a racy access on PREEMPT_RT.
Show original comment 
Yes but this patch has fixes and might get backported, so we need to be use it
is fixing an issue which is real.

Let's try to reason how the race can happen between is_memcg_drain_needed and
drain_stock:

CPU 1						CPU 2

drain_stock
  WRITE_ONCE(stock->nr_pages[i], 0);		is_memcg_drain_needed
  css_put(&old->css);
  /* assume full grace period */
  						  rcu_read_lock()
  						  memcg = READ_ONCE(stock->cached[i]);
  						  if(READ_ONCE(stock->nr_pages[i])
  							mem_cgroup_is_descendant(memcg);

  WRITE_ONCE(stock->cached[i], NULL);


Do you think there is possible race condition in above? I couldn't see.

However I ran this through AI and it has convinced me that on PREEMPT_RT, this
is indeed racy access.

AI reasoning:
-------------

How it breaks on PREEMPT_RT

On RT, local_trylock() takes a per-CPU spinlock_t (an rtmutex). That is
rcu_read_lock() plus migrate_disable(), but the task can still be preempted
inside it.

1. CPU A, refill_stock(X): there is no free slot, so it calls drain_stock(i) on
   old. It sets nr_pages[i] = 0, then css_put(old) drops the last reference and
   queues the release work on CPU A.
2. The refill task is preempted (by a tick or a threaded IRQ) before it clears
   cached[i]. CPU A's kworker runs css_release_work_fn(), which calls call_rcu(),
   and grace period G starts. G will wait for the refill task, because that task is
   inside an RCU reader.
3. CPU B, drain_all_stock() \u2192 is_memcg_drain_needed(): B has already passed a
   quiescent state for G. It enters rcu_read_lock() and loads cached[i] == old. G
   does not wait for this reader, because it started after G did.
4. B is preempted right after that load (RT readers can be preempted).
5. A resumes. It clears cached[i], puts X in slot i with a non-zero nr_pages[i],
   and unlocks. G ends, and old is freed.
6. B resumes. It reads X's non-zero count and calls
   mem_cgroup_is_descendant(old, root). That reads old->css.cgroup from freed
   memory.
View on lore ↗
Signals: requested changes
Joshua Hahn (author) ↳ replying to Shakeel Butt Needs Work LLM
Author acknowledged the feedback as a potential PREEMPT_RT race condition and agreed to restructure the patch in v2, including a new commit message that explains the issue.
Show original comment 
Thanks for taking a look. I think this is the race condition that Sashiko
brought up in the original series that I had.

I will go ahead and send a v2 with a new commit message that explains
this PREEMPT_RT race condition so that it is more obvious where it can
actually race in a kernel. Thanks!

Last comment 2026-10-21 [PATCH v2] mm/memcontrol: publish null pointer before dropping css reference Neutral LLM

This patch addresses a race condition on PREEMPT_RT by publishing the NULL pointer before dropping the css reference in the memcontrol code. The issue arises when the last reference to a cgroup is dropped, and the associated cgroup is freed before the NULL pointer is published. To fix this, the order of operations is reversed so that the NULL pointer is published first.

> <prempt>
> 
> stock->cached[i] is cleared and
> unlocks. Grace period G ends,
> old is freed
> 
> mem_cgroup_is_descendant() reads
> old->css.cgroup which has just
> been freed.
> 
> To close this gap on PREEMPT_RT, switch the order of the referene drop
> and the publishing so that we publish the NULL pointer before dropping
> the reference. Note that this can only happen from drain_stock who can
> hold the last reference, but not on consume_stock since another
> reference will always be held. But fix this in both sites to maintain
> consistency. 
> 
> This is based on a report from Sashiko, who found this pre-existing
> issue in my page_counter_stock series [1].
> 
> [1] https://sashiko.dev/#/patchset/20260928192349.3432886-1-joshua.hahnjy%40gmail.com
> [2] https://lore.kernel.org/all/atfMngq7nyZpt4u-@linux.dev/
> 
> Fixes: e1a366be5cb4 ("mm: memcontrol: switch to rcu protection in drain_all_stock()")
> Signed-off-by: Joshua Hahn <joshua.hahnjy@gmail.com>

Acked-by: Muchun Song <muchun.song@linux.dev>

Last comment 2026-10-22 [PATCH] mm/migrate: wake kcompactd when huge folio migration fails in numa balancing Neutral LLM

This patch addresses a performance issue in NUMA balancing, where large folios fail to migrate due to fragmentation on the destination node. The patch wakes up kcompactd when huge folio migration fails, allowing proactive compaction to help with future migrations.

2 reviewer comments — 0 participants
0 participants
Zi Yan ↳ replying to Joshua Hahn Neutral LLM
Raised questions about the effectiveness of proactive compaction and suggested that kswapd's presence might be a contributing factor to the issue. Suggested considering a manual trigger to wake up proactive reclaim when high-order allocations fail.
Show original comment 
On Thu Oct 22, 2026 at 1:25 PM EDT, Joshua Hahn wrote:
> NUMA balancing migrates a misplaced folio if migrate_balanced_pgdat()
> finds enough headroom on the destination node. For large folios, it then
> allocates a folio from the destination with GFP_TRANSHUGE_LIGHT, which
> wakes up neither kswapd nor kcompactd. This means a hot large folio
> trying to promote to a fragmented destination may never succeed in
> promotion even after repeated retries.
>
> Wake kcompactd on the target node when the destination allocation fails
> for a large folio. Don't wake kswapd, since this path can only be
> reached if there is enough free memory to service the allocation, but it
> is too fragmented to serve the large folio allocation.
>
> This patch has been A/B tested on tiered Meta systems (DRAM + CXL)
> running identical cacheing workloads. Without this patch, we accumulate
> around 4.5k failed THP migrations over the span of a day, while the host
> with this patch accumulates only about 80.

Is proactive compaction enabled? If yes, why does it not help?

>
> Those migration differences leads to about 25% reduced CXL bandwidth
> usage after applying this patch.
>

Hi Zi, thanks for taking a look!

Yes, indeed proactive compaction is enabled with vm.compaction_proactiveness
set to 20. 

Hmm, I wonder why we are seeing this difference then. 

One reason that I can think of is that kswapd is awake very often. My
understanding is that proactive compaction does not run when kswapd is
on, with the idea being that if we can free memory, compaction becomes
easier in the future, so we should try when kswapd is done.

When they overlap, then proactive reclaim is suppressed, so it could be
helpful to have a manual trigger to wake it up when a high order
allocation fails. What do you think? If it would help, I can add this
detail to the commit message as well.

Thanks again for taking a look, Zi. I hope you have a great day!
Joshua

Signals: PROPOSING
[PATCH v7 0/5] mm/page_counter: move stock from mem_cgroup to page_counter 5 patches
Show 5 individual patches
  • [1/5] mm/memcontrol: flatten try_charge_memcg control flow
  • [2/5] mm/page_counter: introduce per-CPU stock
  • [3/5] mm/page_counter: make page_counter_try_charge() stock-aware
  • [4/5] mm/memcontrol: move memory stock to page counters
  • [5/5] mm/memcontrol: add stock to the memsw page counter
Neutral llm-chunked-no-replies

This patch series moves the stock management from mem_cgroup to page_counter, allowing each page_counter to have its own per-CPU cache of pre-charged pages. This change is necessary for future work on scalable and performant page_counters, such as the tiered memcg limits series. The patch preserves the invariant that memory.usage_in_bytes is less than or equal to memory.memsw.usage_in_bytes in cgroup v1, by reporting memory.usage_in_bytes as the smaller of the raw memory and memsw values. This change has no functional impact on memcg v2 users but does affect memcg v1 users by decoupling the memsw and memory stock management.

Last comment 2026-10-19 [PATCH] mm/percpu: clean up callers of pcpu_obj_full_size() Neutral LLM (per-reviewer)

This patch simplifies the callers of pcpu_obj_full_size() by coalescing them to use the total size of a percpu allocation, rather than its size attributable to a single CPU. This change is made in various accounting paths for percpu memory, including memcg pre- and post-allocation hooks, and alloc tag hooks. The patch does not introduce any functional changes and only refactors existing code. It aims to make the intent of using the total allocation size more obvious by reducing the number of places where pcpu_obj_full_size() is called.

Last comment 2026-10-20 Re: [PATCH] mm/memcontrol: publish null pointer before dropping css reference Neutral LLM (per-reviewer)

This patch fixes a rare issue where a memory cgroup (memcg) could be freed while still being referenced by the memcg's stock, leading to a null pointer dereference. The fix involves publishing a NULL pointer before dropping the reference to the cgroup, ensuring that any readers waiting for the RCU grace period are properly notified and can safely access the now-freed memory. This change is based on a report from Sashiko and fixes an issue introduced in a previous patch series by Joshua Hahn.

3 reviewer comments — 0 participants
0 participants
Joshua Hahn (author) Neutral LLM
Author clarified that their original patch was based on an assumption about their own previous code, which actually already addressed the issue by switching the order of reference drop and publishing.
Show original comment 
Oh and just for clarification, my page_counter_stock series actually
gets rid of this codepath and switches the order when moving it anyways.
I just thought I would pull this out early and send it as a fix to see
if it would be worth the effort!
View on lore ↗
Signals: clarification, no clear resolution
Shakeel Butt ↳ replying to Joshua Hahn Inline Review Reviewed-by Needs Work LLM
Reviewer Shakeel Butt questioned the existence of a race condition in the patch, citing a scenario where CPU 1 and CPU 2 can access the same memcg simultaneously due to PREEMPT_RT's behavior with per-CPU spinlocks. He used AI reasoning to conclude that this is indeed a racy access on PREEMPT_RT.
Show original comment 
Yes but this patch has fixes and might get backported, so we need to be use it
is fixing an issue which is real.

Let's try to reason how the race can happen between is_memcg_drain_needed and
drain_stock:

CPU 1						CPU 2

drain_stock
  WRITE_ONCE(stock->nr_pages[i], 0);		is_memcg_drain_needed
  css_put(&old->css);
  /* assume full grace period */
  						  rcu_read_lock()
  						  memcg = READ_ONCE(stock->cached[i]);
  						  if(READ_ONCE(stock->nr_pages[i])
  							mem_cgroup_is_descendant(memcg);

  WRITE_ONCE(stock->cached[i], NULL);


Do you think there is possible race condition in above? I couldn't see.

However I ran this through AI and it has convinced me that on PREEMPT_RT, this
is indeed racy access.

AI reasoning:
-------------

How it breaks on PREEMPT_RT

On RT, local_trylock() takes a per-CPU spinlock_t (an rtmutex). That is
rcu_read_lock() plus migrate_disable(), but the task can still be preempted
inside it.

1. CPU A, refill_stock(X): there is no free slot, so it calls drain_stock(i) on
   old. It sets nr_pages[i] = 0, then css_put(old) drops the last reference and
   queues the release work on CPU A.
2. The refill task is preempted (by a tick or a threaded IRQ) before it clears
   cached[i]. CPU A's kworker runs css_release_work_fn(), which calls call_rcu(),
   and grace period G starts. G will wait for the refill task, because that task is
   inside an RCU reader.
3. CPU B, drain_all_stock() \u2192 is_memcg_drain_needed(): B has already passed a
   quiescent state for G. It enters rcu_read_lock() and loads cached[i] == old. G
   does not wait for this reader, because it started after G did.
4. B is preempted right after that load (RT readers can be preempted).
5. A resumes. It clears cached[i], puts X in slot i with a non-zero nr_pages[i],
   and unlocks. G ends, and old is freed.
6. B resumes. It reads X's non-zero count and calls
   mem_cgroup_is_descendant(old, root). That reads old->css.cgroup from freed
   memory.
View on lore ↗
Signals: requested changes
Joshua Hahn (author) ↳ replying to Shakeel Butt Needs Work LLM
Author acknowledged the feedback as a potential PREEMPT_RT race condition and agreed to restructure the patch in v2, including a new commit message that explains the issue.
Show original comment 
Thanks for taking a look. I think this is the race condition that Sashiko
brought up in the original series that I had.

I will go ahead and send a v2 with a new commit message that explains
this PREEMPT_RT race condition so that it is more obvious where it can
actually race in a kernel. Thanks!

Joshua

  • [9/9] mm/collapse: separate scanning a PTE table from collapsing it Neutral Submitted 2026-10-14Last comment 2026-10-19 Lance Yang David (Arm)
Neutral LLM (per-reviewer)

This patch set separates the collapse engine from its callers, making it more modular and easier to understand. The caller specifies a policy that determines what actions are allowed, rather than relying on who called the function. This change is made by introducing new functions for scanning PTE tables and collapsing them, with the mmap_lock drop moved between these two operations. The patch set includes 9 patches in total, with some of them being standalone and others building upon each other.

Ongoing Submitted 2026-10-09 Last comment 2026-10-19 ⏱ No recent activity [PATCH v3] mm: page_alloc: make defrag_mode retries follow the promoted order Neutral LLM (per-reviewer)

This patch modifies the page allocation mechanism in Linux to make defragmentation mode retries follow the promoted order of requests, rather than the original request order. This change aims to prevent indefinite retrying by order-0 requests and reduce memory pressure caused by frequent direct reclaim runs. The promoted order is computed once per iteration and passed to relevant functions, ensuring consistency across different callers. Additionally, the patch resets retry counters when a promoted request takes the fallback path, allowing for a full retry budget before OOM killing is considered.

From: "Kiryl Shutsemau (Meta)" <kas@kernel.org>
Subject: [PATCH v4] mm: page_alloc: make defrag_mode retries follow the promoted order

Since commit 7e8756d7ad22 ("mm: page_alloc: fix non-movable reclaim
storm in defrag_mode"), direct reclaim and compaction for non-movable
requests under defrag_mode run at pageblock_order, to produce the whole
blocks that ALLOC_NOFRAGMENT needs. The retry decisions that follow
still use the request order. An order-0 request can therefore retry
indefinitely without ever reaching the ALLOC_NOFRAGMENT fallback:

- Reclaim at pageblock_order gives up after one pass as soon as a zone
  looks compaction_ready(), and do_try_to_free_pages() then returns 1
  even though nothing was reclaimed. It returns before the retry that
  would reclaim memory.low-protected cgroups, so when most memory is
  protected, the pass that did run finds next to nothing.

- Compaction at pageblock_order fails or is deferred.

- should_reclaim_retry() takes the reported progress as progress for
  the order-0 request and resets no_progress_loops. The request
  retries.

Order 1-3 requests loop the same way, and should_compact_retry() also
checks their pageblock_order compaction result against the request
order.

On a production host (64G, defrag_mode, memory.low covering most of the
workload), 95% of direct reclaim runs were order-9 runs that returned 1
with nothing reclaimed, at up to 60k runs per second. Across ~200M
should_reclaim_retry() calls in a day, no_progress_loops never left 0.
The spinning allocations were SLUB slab refills for inode and dentry
caches. The time spent registers as memory pressure, and pressure-based
OOM killing takes down both workloads and system services.

For promoted requests:

- Reclaim progress does not reset no_progress_loops, as for costly
  orders.

- should_compact_retry() checks the compaction result at the promoted
  order and does not retry COMPACT_SKIPPED, since the request can fall
  back. The compaction priority floor and the COMPACT_SUCCESS retry
  limit stay those of the request order, so a non-costly request still
  gets its COMPACT_PRIO_SYNC_FULL pass before it falls back.

When a promoted request takes the fallback, reset the retry counters,
so that the fallback attempt gets a full retry budget before the OOM
killer is considered. A request whose reclaim was not promoted spent
its budget on the request itself and keeps its counters, as Usama Arif
pointed out.

__alloc_pages_slowpath() computes the promoted order once per iteration
and keeps it in alloc_context for direct reclaim, direct compaction and
the two retry helpers, so no callee has to recompute it. Harry Yoo asked
for the promoted order to be explicit rather than derived in each
callee, and Vlastimil Babka suggested alloc_context over new function
parameters.

In a VM reproducer (32G, defrag_mode, inode churn under memory.low):

                                        before    after
    should_reclaim_retry() calls           63M     293k
    peak memory pressure (PSI some avg10)  99%      12%

File creation runs 5.7x faster.

Fixes: 7e8756d7ad22 ("mm: page_alloc: fix non-movable reclaim storm in defrag_mode")
Cc: stable@vger.kernel.org
Assisted-by: LLM
Signed-off-by: Kiryl Shutsemau (Meta) <kas@kernel.org>
---
v4:
 - keep the promoted order in alloc_context instead of passing it as a
   new parameter (Vlastimil Babka)

  • [17/18] mm/shmem: read without folio references
  • [18/18] mm/filemap: add a debugfs switch for lockless page cache reads
Neutral LLM (per-reviewer)

This patch series introduces a new mechanism to read from the page cache without taking a reference on folios, which can lead to refcount bouncing between CPUs. The approach involves marking accessed folios as PG_rcu_free and deferring their free until an RCU grace period. This allows for more efficient handling of small reads and reduces the overhead of lock contention. The series includes 18 patches that implement this new mechanism in various parts of the Linux kernel, including filemap_read(), shmem, and memory_failure(). The goal is to improve performance on systems with many CPUs by reducing the number of references taken on folios and minimizing the impact of refcount bouncing.

10 reviewer comments — 0 participants
0 participants
Kiryl (Meta) (author) Positive LLM
The author addressed a concern about contention on the folio's reference count, explaining how PG_rcu_free flag avoids this issue by allowing reads to copy from folios without taking a reference and delaying their free until an RCU grace period. The author confirmed that the flag is only needed for large machines with 64-bit architectures.
Show original comment 
From: Linus Torvalds <torvalds@linux-foundation.org>

Reading a page cache folio without taking a reference would keep CPUs
that read the same file from bouncing its refcount.  But without a
reference the folio can be freed, and its memory reused, while the
reader copies from it.

Add PG_rcu_free: the free of a folio with the flag waits for an RCU
grace period.  A reader that saw the flag under rcu_read_lock(), and
then still found the folio where it looked, can read it until
rcu_read_unlock().  A split copies the flag, as such a reader may still
be reading the new folios.

The folios wait on per-CPU lists, one grace period in flight per CPU:
call_rcu() per folio would queue a callback for every folio of a
truncated file.  A work, which can reschedule, frees each batch.
Reclaim depends on these frees, so the workqueue is WQ_MEM_RECLAIM.

The flag is 64-bit only (CONFIG_PAGE_RCU_FREE): page flags are scarce
on 32-bit, and the contention this avoids is a problem of large
machines.

pgfree_rcu in /proc/vmstat counts the deferred pages.  Nothing sets the
flag yet.

Assisted-by: LLM
Signed-off-by: Linus Torvalds <torvalds@linux-foundation.org>
[kas: rework for flagging at read time, 64-bit only, shorter comments
 and changelog]
Signed-off-by: Kiryl Shutsemau (Meta) <kas@kernel.org>

 #endif /* CONFIG_MEM_ALLOC_PROFILING */
 
+/*
+ * Each CPU has at most one grace period in flight (@queued), covering
+ * @batch.  Folios freed meanwhile wait on @pending for the next one.
+ */
+struct rcu_free_pcpu {
+	spinlock_t lock;
+	bool queued;
+	struct list_head pending;
+	struct list_head batch;
+	struct rcu_work rwork;
+};
+
+static DEFINE_PER_CPU(struct rcu_free_pcpu, rcu_free_pcpu);
+
+static struct workqueue_struct *rcu_free_wq __ro_after_init;
+
+static void rcu_free_start(struct rcu_free_pcpu *r)
+{
+	lockdep_assert_held(&r->lock);
+
+	if (r->queued || list_empty(&r->pending))
+		return;
+
+	list_splice_init(&r->pending, &r->batch);
+	r->queued = true;
+	WARN_ON_ONCE(!queue_rcu_work(rcu_free_wq, &r->rwork));
+}
+
+static void rcu_free_pages(struct list_head *list)
+{
+	struct folio_batch fbatch;
+	struct folio *folio, *next;
+
+	folio_batch_init(&fbatch);
+	list_for_each_entry_safe(folio, next, list, lru) {
+		if (!folio_batch_add(&fbatch, folio)) {
+			free_unref_folios(&fbatch);
+			cond_resched_tasks_rcu_qs();
+		}
+	}
+	if (folio_batch_count(&fbatch))
+		free_unref_folios(&fbatch);
+}
+
+static void rcu_free_workfn(struct work_struct *work)
+{
+	struct rcu_free_pcpu *r = container_of(to_rcu_work(work),
+					       struct rcu_free_pcpu, rwork);
+	LIST_HEAD(done);
+
+	spin_lock_irq(&r->lock);
+	list_splice_init(&r->batch, &done);
+	r->queued = false;
+	rcu_free_start(r);
+	spin_unlock_irq(&r->lock);
+
+	rcu_free_pages(&done);
+}
+
+static void rcu_free_defer(struct folio *folio)
+{
+	struct rcu_free_pcpu *r = raw_cpu_ptr(&rcu_free_pcpu);
+	unsigned long flags;
+
+	spin_lock_irqsave(&r->lock, flags);
+	list_add_tail(&folio->lru, &r->pending);
+	rcu_free_start(r);
+	spin_unlock_irqrestore(&r->lock, flags);
+}
+
+static void __init rcu_free_init(void)
+{
+	int cpu;
+
+	if (!IS_ENABLED(CONFIG_PAGE_RCU_FREE))
+		return;
+
+	rcu_free_wq = alloc_workqueue("rcu_free",
+				      WQ_MEM_RECLAIM | WQ_PERCPU, 0);
+
+	for_each_possible_cpu(cpu) {
+		struct rcu_free_pcpu *r = per_cpu_ptr(&rcu_free_pcpu, cpu);
+
+		spin_lock_init(&r->lock);
+		INIT_LIST_HEAD(&r->pending);
+		INIT_LIST_HEAD(&r->batch);
+		INIT_RCU_WORK(&r->rwork, rcu_free_workfn);
+	}
+}
+

 		unsigned int order, fpi_t fpi_flags)
 {
@@ -1327,6 +1417,19 @@ static __always_inline bool __free_pages_prepare(struct page *page,
 
 	VM_BUG_ON_PAGE(PageTail(page), page);
 
+	/*
+	 * Not for a hwpoisoned order-0 page: it never reaches the allocator,
+	 * and page_handle_poison() takes a reference right after this free.
+	 */
+	if (unlikely(folio_test_rcu_free(folio)) &&
+	    !(PageHWPoison(page) && !order)) {
+		VM_WARN_ON_ONCE(fpi_flags & FPI_NOLOCK);
+		__folio_clear_rcu_free(folio);
+		count_vm_events(PGFREE_RCU, 1 << order);
+		rcu_free_defer(folio);
+		return false;
+	}
+
 	trace_mm_page_free(page, order);
 	kmsan_free_page(page, order);
 
@@ -6401,6 +6504,8 @@ void __init setup_per_cpu_pageset(void)
 	for_each_online_pgdat(pgdat)
 		pgdat->per_cpu_nodestats =
 			alloc_percpu(struct per_cpu_nodestat);
+

Signals: addressed concern, confirmed resolution
Kiryl (Meta) (author) Needs Work LLM
The author addressed a concern about the reuse of stolen page cache pipe buffers, agreeing to drop the try_steal function from page_cache_pipe_buf_ops and modify fuse_try_move_folio() and virtio_console's pipe_to_sg() to copy instead. The author acknowledged that this change is necessary to prevent folios from being reused before they are freed.
Show original comment 
From: Linus Torvalds <torvalds@linux-foundation.org>

PG_rcu_free holds back the reuse of a folio's memory until a grace
period after the folio is freed.  A stolen page cache pipe buffer is
reused without being freed: remove_mapping() takes the folio out of the
page cache and the caller keeps it.  fuse_try_move_folio() puts it into
a fuse file, where writes change it under a reader that found it before
the steal.

Drop ->try_steal from page_cache_pipe_buf_ops.  fuse_try_move_folio()
and virtio_console's pipe_to_sg() then copy, as they already do when
stealing fails.  SPLICE_F_MOVE from the page cache into a fuse file or
a virtio console now copies.

Assisted-by: LLM
Signed-off-by: Linus Torvalds <torvalds@linux-foundation.org>
[kas: rewrote the changelog]
Signed-off-by: Kiryl Shutsemau (Meta) <kas@kernel.org>

Signals: acknowledged a fix is needed
Kiryl (Meta) (author) Needs Work LLM
The author addressed a concern that fuse_try_move_folio() should handle folios marked PG_rcu_free by copying them instead of refusing them, and provided a patch to implement this change.
Show original comment 
fuse_try_move_folio() takes the folio of a pipe buffer and puts it into
a fuse file, where writes change it.  A folio with PG_rcu_free may still
be copied by a lockless page cache read that found it before it left
the page cache.

Such a folio reaches fuse when a mapped file page is gifted with
vmsplice(SPLICE_F_GIFT) and its file is then truncated.
fuse_check_folio() refuses it only because the flag is not among those
it allows, and with a dump_page().

Copy such a folio instead, as fuse does whenever it cannot take one.

Assisted-by: LLM
Signed-off-by: Kiryl Shutsemau (Meta) <kas@kernel.org>

2.54.0
View on lore ↗
Signals: clarification, explanation
Kiryl (Meta) (author) Neutral LLM
The author addressed a concern about the expedited grace period being too aggressive and causing unnecessary work for CPUs that are not running short on memory. They explained that the expedited grace period is only triggered when the zone is below its high watermark or when the pages waiting in it are more than an eighth of its free pages, and that this ensures that the bulk free happens when the memory matters.
Show original comment 
From: Linus Torvalds <torvalds@linux-foundation.org>

A deferred folio waits one to two grace periods on its CPU's list, where
the page allocator cannot use it.  A CPU that frees many flagged folios
at once, for example when a file is truncated, can hold a lot of memory
there.

When more than 4 MiB is pending on a CPU, a work takes both of the CPU's
lists and frees them after an expedited grace period.  That grace period
starts after the take, so it covers every reader that found one of the
folios.  The task that frees them does not wait.

An expedited grace period interrupts every CPU, so keep it for when the
memory matters.  That is when the zone is below its high watermark, or
when the pages waiting in it are more than an eighth of its free pages.
The watermark alone comes too late for a bulk free.  A truncate or
fadvise(POSIX_FADV_DONTNEED) of a hot range defers all of it while the
zone still has memory.  When the readers that refill the range then run
short, nothing is freed that would check again.

With rcu_normal, as on PREEMPT_RT, the expedited grace period is a
normal one.

pgfree_rcu_expedite in /proc/vmstat counts the expedite runs that freed
something.

Assisted-by: LLM
Signed-off-by: Linus Torvalds <torvalds@linux-foundation.org>
[kas: take both lists, expedite only when the memory matters, rewrote
 the changelog]
Signed-off-by: Kiryl Shutsemau (Meta) <kas@kernel.org>

+/* Free what rcu_free_take() took: the grace period starts after that */
+static unsigned long rcu_free_flush(struct list_head *list)
+{
+	if (list_empty(list))
+		return 0;
+
+	synchronize_rcu_expedited();

+static void rcu_free_expedite_workfn(struct work_struct *work)
+{
+	struct rcu_free_pcpu *r = container_of(work, struct rcu_free_pcpu,
+					       expedite);
+	LIST_HEAD(list);
+
+	spin_lock_irq(&r->lock);
+	rcu_free_take(r, &list);
+	spin_unlock_irq(&r->lock);
+
+	if (rcu_free_flush(&list))
+		count_vm_event(PGFREE_RCU_EXPEDITE);

 static void rcu_free_defer(struct folio *folio)
 {
 	struct rcu_free_pcpu *r = raw_cpu_ptr(&rcu_free_pcpu);
 	struct zone *zone = folio_zone(folio);
+	unsigned long free, waiting;
 	unsigned long flags;
 
 	spin_lock_irqsave(&r->lock, flags);
 	__mod_zone_page_state(zone, NR_FREE_RCU_PAGES, folio_nr_pages(folio));
 	list_add_tail(&folio->lru, &r->pending);
+	r->nr_pending += folio_nr_pages(folio);
 	rcu_free_start(r);
 	spin_unlock_irqrestore(&r->lock, flags);
+
+	/* Unlocked: a stale count delays the expedite by one free */
+	if (data_race(r->nr_pending) <= RCU_FREE_EXPEDITE ||
+	    work_pending(&r->expedite))
+		return;
+
+	/*
+	 * An expedited grace period interrupts every CPU: only while the
+	 * zone is short of memory, or while more than an eighth of what it
+	 * has free waits here.
+	 */
+	free = zone_page_state(zone, NR_FREE_PAGES);
+	waiting = zone_page_state(zone, NR_FREE_RCU_PAGES);
+	if (free < high_wmark_pages(zone) || waiting > free / 8)
+		queue_work(rcu_free_wq, &r->expedite);
 }

+		INIT_WORK(&r->expedite, rcu_free_expedite_workfn);
 	}
 }

2.54.0
View on lore ↗
Signals: clarification, explanation
Kiryl (Meta) (author) Neutral LLM
The author addressed a concern about the reclaim path not considering pages waiting for a grace period when deciding whether to retry allocation. They explained that should_reclaim_retry() can hand the allocation to the OOM killer while enough memory is about to come back from these pages, and proposed freeing the waiting pages if they would bring an eligible zone above its min watermark before retrying.
Show original comment 
When reclaim fails, should_reclaim_retry() looks only at free and
reclaimable pages.  It can hand the allocation to the OOM killer while
enough memory is about to come back from the pages that wait for a
grace period.

Before should_reclaim_retry() gives up, free the waiting pages if they
would bring an eligible zone above its min watermark, and retry.  That
costs an expedited grace period, which is cheap next to the OOM killer.
The retries are bounded per no-progress streak, so a drain that does not
help still ends in an OOM kill.

pgfree_rcu_drain in /proc/vmstat counts the drains that freed something.

Assisted-by: LLM
Signed-off-by: Kiryl Shutsemau (Meta) <kas@kernel.org>

+/*
+ * Free what every CPU's lists hold.  A concurrent drain holds the lock
+ * until it has freed what it took.
+ */
+static void rcu_free_drain(void)
+{
+	static DEFINE_MUTEX(lock);
+	LIST_HEAD(list);
+	int cpu;
+
+	mutex_lock(&lock);
+	for_each_possible_cpu(cpu) {
+		struct rcu_free_pcpu *r = per_cpu_ptr(&rcu_free_pcpu, cpu);
+
+		spin_lock_irq(&r->lock);
+		rcu_free_take(r, &list);
+		spin_unlock_irq(&r->lock);
+	}
+	if (rcu_free_flush(&list))
+		count_vm_event(PGFREE_RCU_DRAIN);
+	mutex_unlock(&lock);
+}
+
 static void __init rcu_free_init(void)
 {
 	int cpu;
@@ -4792,6 +4815,52 @@ bool gfp_pfmemalloc_allowed(gfp_t gfp_mask)
 	return !!__gfp_pfmemalloc_flags(gfp_mask);
 }
 
+/*
+ * Reclaim is about to give up: if the pages waiting for a grace period
+ * would bring an eligible zone above its min watermark, free them and
+ * retry.  Not for a dying task, which frees memory by exiting.
+ */
+static bool rcu_free_wait(gfp_t gfp_mask, unsigned int order,
+			  struct alloc_context *ac, int alloc_flags,
+			  int *rcu_free_waits)
+{
+	struct zone *zone;
+	struct zoneref *z;
+
+	if (!IS_ENABLED(CONFIG_PAGE_RCU_FREE))
+		return false;
+	if (tsk_is_oom_victim(current) || fatal_signal_pending(current))
+		return false;
+	if (*rcu_free_waits >= MAX_RECLAIM_RETRIES ||
+	    order > PAGE_ALLOC_COSTLY_ORDER)
+		return false;
+
+	for_each_zone_zonelist_nodemask(zone, z, ac->zonelist,
+				ac->highest_zoneidx, ac->nodemask) {
+		unsigned long waiting, available;
+
+		if (cpusets_enabled() && (alloc_flags & ALLOC_CPUSET) &&
+		    !__cpuset_zone_allowed(zone, gfp_mask))
+			continue;
+
+		waiting = zone_page_state_snapshot(zone, NR_FREE_RCU_PAGES);
+		if (!waiting)
+			continue;
+
+		available = waiting +
+			    zone_page_state_snapshot(zone, NR_FREE_PAGES);
+		if (__zone_watermark_ok(zone, order, min_wmark_pages(zone),
+					ac->highest_zoneidx, alloc_flags,
+					available)) {
+			(*rcu_free_waits)++;
+			rcu_free_drain();
+			return true;
+		}
+	}
+
+	return false;
+}
+

The author addressed a concern that alloc_contig_range() and offline_pages() fail when migrating folios out of a range due to PG_rcu_free waiting for a grace period. The author agreed to restructure the code in v2, adding a check to free waiting folios before proceeding with the migration.
Show original comment 
alloc_contig_range() and offline_pages() migrate the folios out of a
range and then check with test_pages_isolated() that every page is free.
A migrated folio that had PG_rcu_free waits for a grace period after its
free, so the check fails.  alloc_contig_range() returns -EBUSY, and a
CMA or gigantic page allocation can fail; offline_pages() retries until
the grace period ends.

When the check fails and the zone has pages waiting, free them first.
alloc_contig_range() then finds the start of the free range again, as
the freed folio can merge with a free buddy below it.
offline_pages() does so only after a pass that migrated folios: an
offline stuck on a busy page would otherwise start an expedited grace
period on every retry.

Assisted-by: LLM
Signed-off-by: Kiryl Shutsemau (Meta) <kas@kernel.org>
