2.54.0
View on lore ↗
Signals: acknowledged fix needed
Kiryl (Meta) (author) Needs Work LLM
The author addressed a concern that memory_failure() and soft_offline_page() cannot handle pages waiting for an RCU grace period, which have refcount 0 and are not in the buddy allocator. The author agreed to free these pages first when they look like one of them, before setting PG_hwpoison, and also added a wait at the end after setting PG_hwpoison to ensure that readers that started before the page got PG_hwpoison can unmap it.
Show original comment 
A page whose free waits for an RCU grace period has refcount 0 and is
neither on the LRU nor in the buddy allocator.  memory_failure() and
soft_offline_page() cannot handle such a page: get_any_page() retries a
few times and gives up.  Soft offline of the page fails, and a hard
error is ignored.  If the page is part of a large folio, the folio is
later freed with the poisoned page in it, and that page can be handed
out again.

Free the waiting pages first when the page looks like one of them.
memory_failure() has to do it before it sets PG_hwpoison.  The free of a
poisoned order-0 page drops the page instead of returning it to the
buddy allocator, so a later drain would still leave the error ignored.

A folio with PG_rcu_free that is still in use may be copied by a reader
that holds no reference.  When memory_failure() succeeds, its caller can
unmap the page from the direct map under such a reader.  Wait for a
grace period before returning success.  By then the page has left the
page cache, or is an order-0 folio with PG_hwpoison, which a reader that
starts after the grace period has to check.

Wait at the end, not right after setting PG_hwpoison.  A large folio is
split later, and until the new folios are in the page cache, a reader
can still copy the poisoned page as part of the large one.

Both callers hold mf_mutex and can wait for the grace period.

Assisted-by: LLM
Signed-off-by: Kiryl Shutsemau (Meta) <kas@kernel.org>

+/* A page waiting for a grace period is not in the buddy yet: free it */
+static void mf_free_rcu_pages(struct page *p)
+{
+	if (!IS_ENABLED(CONFIG_PAGE_RCU_FREE))
+		return;
+	if (page_count(p))
+		return;
+	if (!zone_page_state_snapshot(page_zone(p), NR_FREE_RCU_PAGES))
+		return;
+	if (is_free_buddy_page(p))
+		return;
+
+	rcu_free_sync();
+}
+

  * The caller must guarantee the folio isn't large folio, except hugetlb.
  * try_to_unmap() can't handle it.

 		goto unlock_mutex;

 	if (TestSetPageHWPoison(p)) {
 		res = -EHWPOISON;
 		if (flags & MF_ACTION_REQUIRED)

+	/*
+	 * The caller may unmap the page once this returns 0.  Lockless
+	 * readers that started before the page got PG_hwpoison, or before a
+	 * split left it in a folio of its own, may still be copying it.

2.54.0
View on lore ↗
Signals: acknowledged fix needed, added code
Kiryl (Meta) (author) Neutral LLM
The author addressed a concern about the folio_mark_accessed() function, which was modified to return whether the folio is hot (i.e., already as accessed as the LRU records). The author explained that this change prepares for reading the folios that it calls hot without a folio reference. No fix is planned in response to this feedback.
Show original comment 
From: Linus Torvalds <torvalds@linux-foundation.org>

Each call of folio_mark_accessed() moves a folio one step up what the
LRU records of its accesses.  Once the folio is at the top, the call
leaves it as it was: the folio is as hot as the LRU can tell.

Return whether the call found the folio there.  On the classic LRU
that is active, referenced and not idle.  On the multi-gen LRU it is
PG_referenced, all the refs bits and PG_workingset, which
lru_gen_inc_refs() now reports.

An unevictable folio counts as hot once it is referenced, and on the
multi-gen LRU from the first call: it stays in memory in any case.  A
dropbehind folio is not hot: it is dropped after the read.

No caller uses the value yet.  Preparation for reading the folios that
it calls hot without a folio reference.

Assisted-by: LLM
Signed-off-by: Linus Torvalds <torvalds@linux-foundation.org>
[kas: shorter changelog]
Signed-off-by: Kiryl Shutsemau (Meta) <kas@kernel.org>

Signals: clarification, explanation
Harry Yoo ↳ replying to Kiryl (Meta) Reviewed-by Needs Work LLM
Harry Yoo noted that applying the patch to hot folios only would limit its benefits to scenarios where small reads contend on the refcount, and suggested considering the scalability issues reported by Willy in a previous discussion about buffered IO limits. Reviewer Harry Yoo pointed out that the patch description does not provide a link to the original RFC by Linus, which is referenced in the series. Reviewer Harry Yoo suggested either improving the heuristic for marking folios with PG_rcu_free to benefit more workloads or identifying specific workloads that heavily contend on a small set of folios' refcounts.
Show original comment 
By applying this to hot folios only, small reads would benefit only when
they contend on the refcount. That further narrows down the scope of
workload that would benefit from this. Random or sequential small reads
still perform the atomics on the refcount.

Looking at what Linus mentioned as "Willy reported real users having
scalability issues in that area a couple of years ago in a discussion
about buffereed IO scalability limits:

  https://lore.kernel.org/all/Zds8T9O4AYAmdS9d@casper.infradead.org/
"

On that thread, from Willy:

---

https://lore.kernel.org/all/Zdz9p_Kn0puI1KEL@casper.infradead.org

Again from willy, from the same thread:

---

from this.

Maybe we should try to either 1) find a better heuristic to mark
which folios should have PG_rcu_free to make it beneficial for wider
range of workloads or 2) identify workload that heavly contends on the
refcount of a small set of folios...

-- 
Cheers,
Harry / Hyeonggon

Re: [PATCH v3] mm: page_alloc: make defrag_mode retries follow the promoted order Neutral llm-chunked-no-replies
Last comment 2026-10-23 Re: [PATCH v3] mm: page_alloc: make defrag_mode retries follow the promoted order Neutral LLM

Patch series to fix defrag_mode retries in page allocation, addressing issues with free order-4+ pages and direct reclaim.

3 reviewer comments — 0 participants
0 participants
Kiryl Shutsemau Neutral LLM
Identified a problem with defrag_mode retries in page allocation, particularly with free order-4+ pages and direct reclaim. Proposed changes to __zone_watermark_ok() to address these issues.
Show original comment 
On Fri, Oct 23, 2026 at 11:59:06AM +0100, Kiryl Shutsemau wrote:
> 64K allocation is still lower with defrag_mode. I will look into this
> further.

Direct reclaim has the same problem.

compaction_ready() and should_continue_reclaim() ask the same watermark
check whether the allocation can already succeed. The free order-4+
pages left in unmovable pageblocks make it say yes, and reclaim stops
early.

I tried to generalize the fix: __zone_watermark_ok() takes the request's
migratetype and, under ALLOC_NOFRAGMENT, does not count other
migratetypes' free pages below pageblock_order. Direct reclaim learns
ALLOC_NOFRAGMENT from the allocation.

Medians of 15 runs of "compact-bench 99 64 16 20 0":

                             defrag_mode=0  defrag_mode=1  defrag_mode=1
                                                           + diff
    compact_stall                    2135          32476          8022
    compact_fail                      668          32330          4880
    64K anon_fault_alloc            20689           6780         19649
    64K anon_fault_fallback           776          14502          1851

The diff below replaces the previous one. It needs cleanup and splitting
before it goes upstream.

Could you give it a try?

Show original comment 
On Fri, Oct 23, 2026 at 11:59:06AM +0100, Kiryl Shutsemau wrote:
> 64K allocation is still lower with defrag_mode. I will look into this
> further.

Direct reclaim has the same problem.

compaction_ready() and should_continue_reclaim() ask the same watermark
check whether the allocation can already succeed. The free order-4+
pages left in unmovable pageblocks make it say yes, and reclaim stops
early.

I tried to generalize the fix: __zone_watermark_ok() takes the request's
migratetype and, under ALLOC_NOFRAGMENT, does not count other
migratetypes' free pages below pageblock_order. Direct reclaim learns
ALLOC_NOFRAGMENT from the allocation.

Medians of 15 runs of "compact-bench 99 64 16 20 0":

                             defrag_mode=0  defrag_mode=1  defrag_mode=1
                                                           + diff
    compact_stall                    2135          32476          8022
    compact_fail                      668          32330          4880
    64K anon_fault_alloc            20689           6780         19649
    64K anon_fault_fallback           776          14502          1851

The diff below replaces the previous one. It needs cleanup and splitting
before it goes upstream.

Could you give it a try?

Patches Submitted (3)
[RFC PATCH v6 12/12] mm, swap: back vswap clusters with a VM_SPARSE array
Show 2 individual patches
  • [10/1] mm, swap: defer memcg_table allocation for physical swap clusters
  • [11/1] mm, swap: add vmstat and memcg counters for vswap
Neutral llm-chunked-no-replies

This patch replaces vswap's cluster_info storage from an xarray of individually allocated clusters to a VM_SPARSE vmalloc array, simplifying access and reducing memory allocation overhead. The change eliminates the need for per-cluster RCU grace periods and reduces the size of swap clusters. It also removes the wrapper function swap_cluster_info_dynamic and merges the two cluster arrays into si->cluster_info. As a result, swap clusters are no longer destroyed when they empty, but instead wait to be reused like physical devices' clusters.

Last comment 2026-10-20 [PATCH] mm, swap: revalidate populated clusters before scanning Neutral LLM

This patch addresses a potential race condition in the swap cluster allocation process by revalidating populated clusters before scanning them. It ensures that the cluster table is not published until the lock is reacquired, preventing a NULL pointer dereference when the resumed scan tries to access the table.

1 reviewer comment — 0 participants
0 participants
Youngjun Park Reviewed-by Positive LLM
Identified a potential race condition in the swap cluster allocation process and suggested that publishing the table only after reacquiring the cluster lock is correct. Agreed with Kemeng's suggestion to reject unusable clusters under the lock.

Last comment 2026-10-21 [PATCH v6 00/12] Virtual Swap Space (Swap Table Edition) 12 patches
Show 9 individual patches
  • [01/12] mm, swap: add virtual swap device infrastructure
  • [02/12] mm, swap: support zswap and zero-filled swap pages as vswap backends
  • [03/12] mm, swap: prepare the swap IO path for vswap
  • [04/12] mm, swap: support physical swap as a vswap backend
  • [05/12] mm, swap: enable THP swapin for vswap entries
  • [06/12] mm, swap: write back vswap zswap entries to physical swap
  • [07/12] mm, swap: reclaim physical slots backing cache-only vswap entries
  • [08/12] mm, swap: only charge physical swap entries
  • [09/12] mm, swap: add debugfs counters for vswap
Neutral LLM (per-reviewer)

This patch series introduces Virtual Swap Space, a feature that allows for virtual swap space allocation within the Linux kernel. It enables the use of zswap (a compressed block device) as a backing store for virtual swap space, allowing for more efficient use of physical memory and reducing the need for traditional swap partitions. The patches include various cleanups, bug fixes, and improvements to existing code, including changes to cgroup memory statistics and sysctl parameters. The goal is to provide a flexible and scalable solution for managing swap space in modern Linux systems.

10 reviewer comments — 0 participants
0 participants
Nhat Pham (author) Needs Work LLM
The author addressed concerns about locking in the swapoff path, agreeing to restructure in v2 to drop the per-vswap spinlock before calling try_to_unmap().
Show original comment 
Create a virtual swap device (just under 8 TiB with 4 KiB pages), along
with the dynamic cluster infrastructure that the rest of the vswap layer is
built on. swap_cluster_info_dynamic keeps per-cluster info in an xarray, so
a device can be sized without a static cluster_info[] array. For now, vswap
requires a 64-bit architecture.

The dynamic-cluster allocator is wired in, but nothing reaches it yet.
vswap_si is kept off the swap device lists, and no allocation path can
select it. Backends (zswap, zero, physical disk) and the vswap-aware
swap-out / swap-in / writeback paths arrive in subsequent patches.

Routing is controlled by the "vswap=" kernel parameter, defaulting to
CONFIG_VSWAP_DEFAULT_ON. When off, no device exists, every vswap path is
skipped, and swap behavior is unchanged. When on, vswap_init() creates
the device and enables a static key only after it is fully published.
The device lives for the lifetime of the kernel and cannot be swapon'd
or swapoff'd.

The device is capped at REFCOUNT_MAX pages, just under 8 TiB with 4 KiB
pages. Every swapped page takes a reference on its cgroup's
objcg->private_id_ref, a 32-bit refcount_t, so a cgroup that swaps past
that saturates it and leaks the objcg until reboot. The limit is not
specific to vswap, a physical swapfile that large hits it too.

Suggested-by: Kairui Song <kasong@tencent.com>
Signed-off-by: Nhat Pham <nphamcs@gmail.com>

+config VSWAP_DEFAULT_ON
+	bool "Route swapouts through virtual swap by default"
+	depends on SWAP && 64BIT
+	default n
+	help
+	  Virtual swap allows zswap and zero-filled pages to be used
+	  without swapping on a physical device first, and lets a page
+	  move between zswap and a swapfile without invalidating the page
+	  table entries that refer to it.
+
+	  Swap entries are handed out by a virtual swap device instead of
+	  naming a slot on a real one, so the backing can be chosen and
+	  changed after the entry exists.
+
+	  Say Y to make "vswap=on" the default, routing swapouts through
+	  the virtual swap layer from boot.
+
+	  Say N (default) to leave vswap off unless "vswap=on" is passed
+	  on the kernel command line.

+static inline struct swap_cluster_info *__vswap_cluster_lock(
+		struct swap_info_struct *si, unsigned long offset, bool irq)
+{
+	struct swap_cluster_info *ci;
+
+	rcu_read_lock();
+	ci = __swap_offset_to_cluster(si, offset);
+	if (ci) {
+		if (irq)
+			spin_lock_irq(&ci->lock);
+		else
+			spin_lock(&ci->lock);
+
+		/* The cluster can be torn down while we wait for the lock. */
+		if (ci->flags == CLUSTER_FLAG_DEAD) {
+			if (irq)
+				spin_unlock_irq(&ci->lock);
+			else
+				spin_unlock(&ci->lock);
+			ci = NULL;
+		}
+	}
+	rcu_read_unlock();
+	return ci;

+/*
+ * Resolve @entry's cluster and read its slot, both under RCU. A vswap
+ * cluster is allocated on demand and freed by kfree_rcu(), so a caller
+ * starting from an entry cannot resolve it beforehand.
+ */
+static inline unsigned long swap_table_lookup(swp_entry_t entry)
+{
+	struct swap_cluster_info *ci;
+	atomic_long_t *table;
+	unsigned long swp_tb;
+
+	rcu_read_lock();
+	ci = __swap_entry_to_cluster(entry);
+	if (!ci) {
+		rcu_read_unlock();
+		return null_to_swp_tb();
+	}
+	table = rcu_dereference(ci->table);
+	swp_tb = table ? atomic_long_read(&table[swp_cluster_offset(entry)])
+		       : null_to_swp_tb();
+	rcu_read_unlock();
+
+	return swp_tb;
+}

+static unsigned int vswap_alloc_cluster(struct swap_info_struct *si,
+					struct folio *folio)
+{
+	struct swap_cluster_info_dynamic *ci_dyn;
+	struct swap_cluster_info *ci;
+	unsigned long offset;
+
+	VM_WARN_ON(!swap_is_vswap(si));
+
+	ci_dyn = kzalloc_obj(*ci_dyn, GFP_ATOMIC | __GFP_NOWARN);
+	if (!ci_dyn)
+		return SWAP_ENTRY_INVALID;
+
+	spin_lock_init(&ci_dyn->ci.lock);
+	INIT_LIST_HEAD(&ci_dyn->ci.list);
+
+	if (swap_cluster_alloc_table(&ci_dyn->ci, GFP_ATOMIC | __GFP_NOWARN)) {
+		kfree(ci_dyn);
+		return SWAP_ENTRY_INVALID;
+	}
+
+	/* Lock before publishing: xa_alloc makes the cluster findable by offset. */
+	ci = &ci_dyn->ci;
+	spin_lock(&ci->lock);
+
+	if (xa_alloc(&si->cluster_info_pool, &ci_dyn->index, ci_dyn,
+		     XA_LIMIT(1, DIV_ROUND_UP(si->max, SWAPFILE_CLUSTER) - 1),
+		     GFP_ATOMIC | __GFP_NOWARN)) {
+		spin_unlock(&ci->lock);
+		swap_cluster_free_table(&ci_dyn->ci);
+		kfree(ci_dyn);
+		return SWAP_ENTRY_INVALID;
+	}
+
+	offset = cluster_offset(si, ci);
+	return alloc_swap_scan_cluster(si, ci, folio, offset);
+}

+static const struct swap_ops vswap_ops = { };

+static int __init vswap_init(void)
+{
+	struct swap_info_struct *si;
+	unsigned long maxpages;
+	int err;
+
+	if (!IS_ENABLED(CONFIG_64BIT)) {
+		if (vswap_enabled_early)
+			pr_warn("vswap: requires 64-bit architecture; vswap disabled, swapout falls back to direct physical swap\n");
+		return 0;
+	}
+
+	if (!vswap_enabled_early)
+		return 0;
+
+	si = alloc_swap_info();
+	if (IS_ERR(si)) {
+		pr_warn("vswap: alloc_swap_info failed (%ld); vswap disabled, swapout falls back to direct physical swap\n",
+			PTR_ERR(si));
+		return 0;
+	}
+
+	/*
+	 * Each swapped page holds a reference on its cgroup's
+	 * objcg->private_id_ref, a 32-bit refcount_t, so a cgroup that swaps
+	 * REFCOUNT_MAX pages saturates it and leaks the objcg until reboot.
+	 * Cap the device there.
+	 */
+	maxpages = min(swapfile_maximum_size,
+		       ALIGN_DOWN((unsigned long)REFCOUNT_MAX, SWAPFILE_CLUSTER));
+	/*
+	 * SWP_WRITEOK enables slot allocation. SWP_SOLIDSTATE selects
+	 * per-CPU cluster allocation; vswap has no si->global_cluster.
+	 */
+	si->flags |= SWP_VSWAP | SWP_SOLIDSTATE | SWP_WRITEOK;
+	si->ops = &vswap_ops;
+	si->bdev = NULL;
+	si->max = maxpages;
+	si->pages = maxpages - 1;
+
+	INIT_WORK(&si->discard_work, swap_discard_work);
+	INIT_WORK(&si->reclaim_work, swap_reclaim_work);
+
+	err = setup_swap_clusters_info(si, NULL, maxpages);
+	if (err)
+		goto fail;
+
+	mutex_lock(&swapon_mutex);
+	enable_swap_info(si);
+	mutex_unlock(&swapon_mutex);
+
+	vswap_si = si;
+	pr_info("vswap: created virtual swap device (%lu pages)\n", maxpages);
+
+	/* Last: everything above must be visible before routing starts. */
+	static_branch_enable(&vswap_key);
+	return 0;
+
+fail:
+	pr_warn("vswap: setup_swap_clusters_info failed (%d); vswap disabled, swapout falls back to direct physical swap\n",
+		err);
+	spin_lock(&swap_lock);
+	si->flags = 0;
+	spin_unlock(&swap_lock);
+	return 0;
+}
+late_initcall(vswap_init);

2.53.0-Meta
View on lore ↗
Signals: acknowledged a fix is needed
Nhat Pham (author) Positive LLM
The author addressed a concern about the swap IO path not being able to submit IO for a vswap folio backed by a physical slot, which identifies neither the backing device nor sector. The author modified the code to compute and store the sector from an explicit entry (swap_entry_sector) in various functions, including swap_add_folio, __swap_writeout, and ops->can_merge. This change allows the IO path to address the IO from the stored sector rather than folio->swap.
Show original comment 
In preparation for adding a physical swap backend for vswap, make the
swap IO path able to submit IO for a swap entry other than folio->swap.

The swap IO path derives the target device and sector from folio->swap.
For a vswap folio backed by a physical slot that entry is virtual, so it
identifies neither the backing device nor the sector to submit IO
against.

Compute the sector from an explicit entry (swap_folio_sector becomes
swap_entry_sector), thread that entry through swap_add_folio,
__swap_writeout and ops->can_merge, and stash it in swap_iocb so the
submit and completion paths address the IO from it rather than from
folio->swap.

Carry reclaim's NOFS restriction in the I/O context. A subsequent patch
uses it when zswap_store() fails and writeout falls back to physical swap.

ops->can_merge changes anchor with it: swap_bdev_can_merge() compares
sectors and swap_fs_can_merge() byte offsets, and both now measure from the
batch head plus the accumulated length instead of from the previous folio
plus its size. The two agree at every step, since a batch only grows
through merges that passed the same test. The blkg comparison is unchanged
and still anchors on the last folio in the batch.

All callers pass folio->swap, so there is no functional change.

Signed-off-by: Nhat Pham <nphamcs@gmail.com>

2.53.0-Meta
View on lore ↗
Signals: author provided a clear solution to the issue
Nhat Pham (author) Needs Work LLM
The author is addressing concerns about the reclaim and overcommit logic for vswap, specifically how to handle physical swapfile-less swapping. They acknowledge that anon reclaim gating needs a change to accommodate vswap zswap-backed swapout, and propose adding a new function mem_cgroup_can_swap() to determine whether a cgroup can swap within its headroom. The author also suggests changing the OVERCOMMIT_GUESS heuristic to reference RAM rather than totalram_pages() + total_swap_pages when vswap is enabled.
Show original comment 
This patch adds support for zswap and zero-filled swap backends for vswap.

This allows users to use zswap without a physical backing swapfile.

If zswap_store fails, the page stays dirty in the swap cache. Zswap
writeback of vswap-backed entries is also disabled: they have no physical
slot to write back to yet, so the zswap shrinker is skipped while vswap is
enabled.

THP swapin is disabled for vswap entries for now.

We also need to change the reclaim and overcommit logic to accommodate
physical swapfile-less swapping:

* Anon reclaim gating needs a matching change: a vswap zswap-backed
  swapout consumes no physical slot, so the physical free count no longer
  tells reclaim whether anon is reclaimable. Add mem_cgroup_can_swap(),
  which answers that question: within the cgroup's swap.max headroom,
  either zswap will take the folio or a physical slot is free.

* The OVERCOMMIT_GUESS heuristic gates a single allocation on
  totalram_pages() + total_swap_pages. Vswap deliberately contributes to
  neither, so on a host with no swapfile the bound collapses to RAM alone
  and allocations that vswap could absorb are refused. Compression swap is
  backed by memory rather than by a device, so reference the bound to RAM:
  add 2 * totalram_pages() when vswap and zswap are both on, for a 3x RAM
  ceiling plus whatever physical swap exists.

Suggested-by: Kairui Song <kasong@tencent.com>
Signed-off-by: Nhat Pham <nphamcs@gmail.com>

+/**
+ * mem_cgroup_can_swap - can @memcg swap out at least @nr_pages more pages?
+ * @memcg: the memcg to query
+ * @nr_pages: the number of pages the caller wants to swap out
+ *
+ * Return: true if @memcg can swap out at least @nr_pages more pages.
+ */
+bool mem_cgroup_can_swap(struct mem_cgroup *memcg, long nr_pages)
+{
+	if (!mem_cgroup_disabled() && !do_memsw_account() &&
+	    page_counter_margin(&memcg->swap) < nr_pages)
+		return false;
+
+	return mem_cgroup_can_vswap(memcg) || get_nr_swap_pages() >= nr_pages;
+}

+bool mem_cgroup_can_vswap(struct mem_cgroup *memcg)
+{
+	return vswap_is_enabled() && zswap_is_enabled() &&
+	       (!memcg || mem_cgroup_may_zswap(memcg, false));
+}
+
+static bool folio_may_vswap(struct folio *folio)
+{
+	struct obj_cgroup *objcg;
+	bool may_zswap;
+
+	if (!vswap_is_enabled() || !zswap_is_enabled())
+		return false;
+
+	/*
+	 * If zswap will not take the folio, writeout has to find a physical
+	 * slot anyway. We are just incurring indirection overhead
+	 * unnecessarily.
+	 */
+	objcg = get_obj_cgroup_from_folio(folio);
+	may_zswap = !objcg || obj_cgroup_may_zswap(objcg);
+	if (objcg)
+		obj_cgroup_put(objcg);
+	return may_zswap;
+}
+
+static bool vswap_alloc(struct folio *folio)
+{
+	unsigned int order = folio_order(folio);
+	struct swap_cluster_info *ci;
+	unsigned long offset;
+
+	local_lock(&percpu_vswap_cluster.lock);
+	offset = this_cpu_read(percpu_vswap_cluster.offset[order]);
+
+	if (offset != SWAP_ENTRY_INVALID) {
+		ci = swap_cluster_lock(vswap_si, offset);
+		if (ci && cluster_is_usable(ci, order)) {
+			if (cluster_is_empty(ci))
+				offset = cluster_offset(vswap_si, ci);
+			alloc_swap_scan_cluster(vswap_si, ci, folio, offset);
+		} else if (ci) {
+			swap_cluster_unlock(ci);
+		}
+	}
+
+	if (!folio_test_swapcache(folio))
+		cluster_alloc_swap_entry(vswap_si, folio);
+
+	if (folio_test_swapcache(folio)) {
+		/* alloc_swap_scan_cluster updated percpu offset already */
+		local_unlock(&percpu_vswap_cluster.lock);
+		return true;
+	}
+
+	this_cpu_write(percpu_vswap_cluster.offset[order], SWAP_ENTRY_INVALID);
+	local_unlock(&percpu_vswap_cluster.lock);
+	return false;
+}
+

+/**
+ * __vswap_release_backing - release the backing of a range of vtable slots
+ * @ci: the locked vswap cluster
+ * @ci_start: first slot offset within @ci
+ * @nr: number of slots
+ *
+ * Releases the backing of each slot in [@ci_start, @ci_start + @nr).
+ * Clears the zero marks if set.
+ *
+ * Context: caller must hold @ci->lock.
+ */
+void __vswap_release_backing(struct swap_cluster_info *ci,
+			     unsigned int ci_start, unsigned int nr)
+{
+	struct swap_cluster_info_dynamic *ci_dyn;
+	unsigned int ci_off;
+	unsigned long vt;
+
+	lockdep_assert_held(&ci->lock);
+	ci_dyn = container_of(ci, struct swap_cluster_info_dynamic, ci);
+
+	for (ci_off = ci_start; ci_off < ci_start + nr; ci_off++) {
+		vt = __vtable_get(ci_dyn, ci_off);
+
+		switch (vtable_type(vt)) {
+		case VSWAP_ZSWAP:
+			zswap_entry_free(vtable_to_zswap(vt));
+			break;
+		case VSWAP_NONE:
+			break;
+		default:
+			/* VSWAP_ZERO/VSWAP_FOLIO are return-only, not vtable tags */
+			break;
+		}
+
+		__vtable_set(ci_dyn, ci_off, VSWAP_NONE);
+		/* Zero-backed state lives in swap_table; clear it too. */
+		if (__swap_table_test_zero(ci, ci_off))
+			__swap_table_clear_zero(ci, ci_off);
+	}
+}
+
+/**
+ * folio_release_vswap_backing() - Drop all backing for a folio's vswap entry.
+ * @folio: the folio, occupying a virtual swap entry.
+ *
+ * Release whatever backing the folio's virtual swap slots currently hold and
+ * reset them to empty, so a fresh backing can be installed. Used when a
+ * folio's swap backend is replaced.
+ *
+ * Context: Caller must hold the folio lock; @folio must be in the swap cache
+ * and occupy a virtual swap entry.
+ */
+void folio_release_vswap_backing(struct folio *folio)
+{
+	struct swap_cluster_info *ci;
+	int nr = folio_nr_pages(folio);
+	unsigned int voff;
+
+	ci = __swap_entry_to_cluster(folio->swap);
+	voff = swp_cluster_offset(folio->swap);
+
+	spin_lock(&ci->lock);
+	__vswap_release_backing(ci, voff, nr);
+	spin_unlock(&ci->lock);
+}
+

  * @folio: folio with swap entries bounded.

diff --git a/mm/vswap.h b/mm/vswap.h
index 16395f357955..5334c77b6b84 100644
--- a/mm/vswap.h
+++ b/mm/vswap.h
@@ -11,8 +11,22 @@
 #include <linux/swap.h>
 #include "swap.h"
 
+struct zswap_entry;
+
+/*
+ * VSWAP_ZERO and VSWAP_FOLIO are return-only values synthesized from
+ * swap_table state; the rest are stored in the vtable per slot.
+ */
+enum vswap_backing_type {
+	VSWAP_NONE	= 0,
+	VSWAP_ZSWAP	= 1,
+	VSWAP_ZERO,
+	VSWAP_FOLIO,
+};
+
 #ifdef CONFIG_SWAP
 
+#include "swap_table.h"
 DECLARE_STATIC_KEY_FALSE(vswap_key);
 
 /*
@@ -29,6 +43,177 @@ static inline bool is_vswap_entry(swp_entry_t entry)
 	return swap_is_vswap(__swap_entry_to_info(entry));
 }
 
+/*
+ * Virtual table entry encoding for vswap clusters.
+ *
+ * Each entry in ci_dyn->virtual_table stores the backing type and
+ * pointer for a virtual swap slot. Tag in low 3 bits, payload in
+ * upper 61 bits.
+ *
+ *   NONE:   |----- 0000 ------|000|  - no separate backend pointer
+ *   ZSWAP:  |--- zswap_entry* |001|  - compressed in zswap (tag in low bits)
+ *
+ * Pointer payloads (ZSWAP) are stored directly with the tag OR'd into the
+ * low bits (kernel pointers are >= 8-byte aligned, same approach as xarray).
+ *
+ * vtable[i] = NONE does not by itself mean "free". The swap_table entry
+ * and the per-slot zero flag carry the rest of the state. The full
+ * per-slot state table is:
+ *
+ *   vtable[i] | swap_table[i] | zero  | meaning
+ *   ----------+---------------+-------+--------------------------------
+ *   NONE      | NULL          | clear | truly free / unbacked
+ *   NONE      | PFN           | clear | folio cached, no backing
+ *   NONE      | shadow        | clear | evicted, no backing: data lost
+ *   NONE      | *             | set   | zero-backed; cached if PFN set
+ *   ZSWAP     | PFN           | clear | folio cached + zswap entry
+ *   ZSWAP     | shadow / NULL | clear | evicted, only in zswap
+ *
+ * Locking: a slot's vtable entry (the vswap entry's backend) is only
+ * stable while the caller owns and holds the lock on that entry's swap
+ * cache folio. The cluster lock (ci_dyn->ci.lock) only makes an individual
+ * vtable read atomic, and by itself does not give the caller the right to
+ * change the backend. A backend read without the folio lock is
+ * best-effort and must be re-validated under the folio lock before
+ * being acted on.
+ *
+ * Zero-backed slots use the swap_table per-slot zero flag (same as
+ * direct-mapped physical swap), via __swap_table_test_zero() and friends,
+ * which fall back to ci->zero_bitmap where the flag does not fit. Cached
+ * folios are read out of the swap_table PFN entry; there is no separate FOLIO
+ * vtable type because the folio pointer would duplicate that PFN and
+ * would go stale on folio migration / split.
+ */
+
+#define VTABLE_TAG_BITS		3
+#define VTABLE_TAG_MASK		((1UL << VTABLE_TAG_BITS) - 1)
+
+static inline enum vswap_backing_type vtable_type(unsigned long vt)
+{
+	return vt & VTABLE_TAG_MASK;
+}
+
+static inline struct zswap_entry *vtable_to_zswap(unsigned long vt)
+{
+	VM_WARN_ON(vtable_type(vt) != VSWAP_ZSWAP);
+	return (struct zswap_entry *)(vt & ~VTABLE_TAG_MASK);
+}
+
+/* Virtual table accessors */
+
+static inline unsigned long __vtable_get(struct swap_cluster_info_dynamic *ci_dyn,
+					 unsigned int off)
+{
+	VM_WARN_ON_ONCE(off >= SWAPFILE_CLUSTER);
+	return atomic_long_read(&ci_dyn->virtual_table[off]);
+}
+
+static inline void __vtable_set(struct swap_cluster_info_dynamic *ci_dyn,
+				unsigned int off, unsigned long vt)
+{
+	VM_WARN_ON_ONCE(off >= SWAPFILE_CLUSTER);
+	atomic_long_set(&ci_dyn->virtual_table[off], vt);
+}
+
+/**
+ * vswap_lock_cluster - look up and lock the vswap cluster for an entry
+ * @entry: the virtual swap entry
+ * @voff: out param, receives @entry's slot offset within the cluster
+ *
+ * Return: the locked vswap cluster, or NULL if @entry has no live cluster.
+ */
+static inline struct swap_cluster_info_dynamic *
+vswap_lock_cluster(swp_entry_t entry, unsigned int *voff)
+{
+	struct swap_cluster_info *ci;
+
+	ci = swap_cluster_lock(__swap_entry_to_info(entry), swp_offset(entry));
+	if (!ci)
+		return NULL;
+	*voff = swp_cluster_offset(entry);
+	return container_of(ci, struct swap_cluster_info_dynamic, ci);
+}
+
+void __vswap_release_backing(struct swap_cluster_info *ci,
+			     unsigned int ci_start, unsigned int nr);
+
+/**
+ * vswap_zswap_store - record a zswap entry as the backing for a vswap entry.
+ * @entry: the vswap entry
+ * @ze: the zswap entry now holding @entry's compressed data
+ *
+ * Releases @entry's previous backing, and sets the zswap entry @ze as the new
+ * backing.
+ *
+ * Context: takes and drops the vswap cluster lock internally.
+ */
+static inline void vswap_zswap_store(swp_entry_t entry,
+				     struct zswap_entry *ze)
+{
+	struct swap_cluster_info_dynamic *ci_dyn;
+	unsigned int voff;
+
+	ci_dyn = vswap_lock_cluster(entry, &voff);
+	__vswap_release_backing(&ci_dyn->ci, voff, 1);
+	__vtable_set(ci_dyn, voff, (unsigned long)ze | VSWAP_ZSWAP);
+	swap_cluster_unlock(&ci_dyn->ci);
+}
+
+/**
+ * vswap_zswap_load - return the zswap entry backing a vswap entry
+ * @entry: the virtual swap entry
+ *
+ * Context: takes and drops the vswap cluster lock internally.
+ * Return: the backing zswap entry, or NULL if @entry is not zswap-backed.
+ */
+static inline struct zswap_entry *vswap_zswap_load(swp_entry_t entry)
+{
+	struct swap_cluster_info_dynamic *ci_dyn;
+	unsigned int voff;
+	unsigned long vt;
+
+	ci_dyn = vswap_lock_cluster(entry, &voff);
+	if (!ci_dyn)
+		return NULL;
+	vt = __vtable_get(ci_dyn, voff);
+	swap_cluster_unlock(&ci_dyn->ci);
+
+	if (vtable_type(vt) != VSWAP_ZSWAP)
+		return NULL;
+	return vtable_to_zswap(vt);
+}
+
+void folio_release_vswap_backing(struct folio *folio);
+
+static inline int vswap_cluster_alloc_vtable(struct swap_cluster_info_dynamic *ci_dyn,
+					     gfp_t gfp)
+{
+	ci_dyn->virtual_table = kcalloc(SWAPFILE_CLUSTER,
+					sizeof(*ci_dyn->virtual_table), gfp);
+	return ci_dyn->virtual_table ? 0 : -ENOMEM;
+}
+
+static inline void vswap_cluster_free_vtable(struct swap_cluster_info *ci)
+{
+	struct swap_cluster_info_dynamic *ci_dyn;
+
+	ci_dyn = container_of(ci, struct swap_cluster_info_dynamic, ci);
+	kfree(ci_dyn->virtual_table);
+	ci_dyn->virtual_table = NULL;
+}
+
+#else /* !CONFIG_SWAP */
+
+static inline bool vswap_is_enabled(void)
+{
+	return false;
+}
+
+static inline bool is_vswap_entry(swp_entry_t entry)
+{
+	return false;
+}
+
 #endif /* CONFIG_SWAP */
 
 #endif /* _MM_VSWAP_H */

2.53.0-Meta
View on lore ↗
Signals: acknowledges fix needed, proposes changes
Nhat Pham (author) Neutral LLM
The author addressed a concern about the swapoff path dropping the per-vswap spinlock before calling try_to_unmap(), explaining that dirtying would write uninitialized memory out to swap and instead reports SIGBUS on the next fault.
Show original comment 
Add physical swap as a backend for the virtual swap layer. When zswap
declines a page, the swapout path allocates a physical slot on demand
for swap out.

Each vswap entry's physical slot is tracked via a vswap-tagged
swap_table entry on the physical cluster (an rmap back to the vswap
entry).

swapoff reads each vswap entry back through its rmap slot before freeing
the physical slot. A failed read leaves the folio not uptodate, so drop
it instead of marking it dirty: dirtying would write uninitialised memory
out to swap, and the loss is now reported as SIGBUS on the next fault
rather than silently returning stale data.

If zswap is disabled at the host level, or the folio's cgroup is at its
zswap limit, the folio still gets a physical swap slot, bypassing vswap
and mapping the slot directly into the PTEs. In practice the swapfile
backend is only reached when zswap declines the folio at swap_writeout()
time, most often because the pool is full.

Zswap writeback to physical swap is added in a following patch. Reclaim of
physical slots backing cache-only vswap entries follows it.

Suggested-by: Kairui Song <kasong@tencent.com>
Signed-off-by: Nhat Pham <nphamcs@gmail.com>

+/*
+ * Vswap-tagged swap table entry: rmap for vswap-backing physical slots.
+ *
+ * On physical clusters, a Vswap-tagged entry stores the offset of the
+ * vswap entry that owns this physical slot (the reverse map). Only the
+ * offset is stored; the swap type is implicit (always vswap_si->type,
+ * since there is exactly one vswap device).
+ *
+ *   Vswap:    |---- vswap offset ----|100|
+ */
+#define SWP_TB_VSWAP_MARK_BITS	3
+#define SWP_TB_VSWAP_MARK	0b100UL
+#define SWP_TB_VSWAP_MARK_MASK	((1UL << SWP_TB_VSWAP_MARK_BITS) - 1)
+#define SWP_RMAP_ENTRY_MASK	(~SWP_TB_VSWAP_MARK_MASK)
+
+static inline bool swp_tb_is_vswap(unsigned long swp_tb)
+{
+	return (swp_tb & SWP_TB_VSWAP_MARK_MASK) == SWP_TB_VSWAP_MARK;
+}
+
+static inline unsigned long swp_entry_to_swp_tb_vswap(swp_entry_t entry)
+{
+	return (swp_offset(entry) << SWP_TB_VSWAP_MARK_BITS) | SWP_TB_VSWAP_MARK;
+}
+
+static inline swp_entry_t swp_tb_vswap_to_swp_entry(unsigned long swp_tb)
+{
+	unsigned long offset;
+
+	VM_WARN_ON(!swp_tb_is_vswap(swp_tb));
+	offset = (swp_tb & SWP_RMAP_ENTRY_MASK) >> SWP_TB_VSWAP_MARK_BITS;
+	return swp_entry(vswap_si->type, offset);
+}
+
 #endif

+/**
+ * folio_release_non_phys_swap_backing() - Drop a folio's non-physical vswap backing.
+ * @folio: the folio, occupying a virtual swap entry.
+ *
+ * Release the zswap backing recorded for @folio's virtual swap entry,
+ * leaving the slots empty so the writeout path can install fresh physical
+ * backing. Does nothing when the entry is already backed by physical
+ * swapfile slots, which are kept for reuse, or when it has no backing
+ * beyond the swap cache folio itself.
+ *
+ * Context: Caller must hold the folio lock; @folio must be in the swap cache
+ * and occupy a virtual swap entry.
+ */
+void folio_release_non_phys_swap_backing(struct folio *folio)
+{
+	struct swap_cluster_info *ci;
+	struct swap_cluster_info_dynamic *ci_dyn;
+	int nr = folio_nr_pages(folio);
+	unsigned int voff;
+	unsigned long vt;
+	enum vswap_backing_type type;
+
+	ci = __swap_entry_to_cluster(folio->swap);
+	ci_dyn = container_of(ci, struct swap_cluster_info_dynamic, ci);
+	voff = swp_cluster_offset(folio->swap);
+
+	spin_lock(&ci->lock);
+	/*
+	 * A folio's slots cannot mix swapfile with other backends, except
+	 * mid-backend-change, which always starts from slot 0.
+	 */
+	vt = __vtable_get(ci_dyn, voff);
+	type = vtable_type(vt);
+
+	if (type == VSWAP_SWAPFILE || type == VSWAP_NONE) {
+		spin_unlock(&ci->lock);
+		return;
+	}
+
+	__vswap_release_backing(ci, voff, nr);
+	spin_unlock(&ci->lock);
+}
+
+/**
+ * folio_realloc_swap() - Back a virtual swap folio with a physical swap slot.
+ * @folio: the folio, occupying a virtual swap entry.
+ *
+ * Ensure @folio's virtual swap entry has physical (swapfile) backing,
+ * allocating a physical slot on demand if it has none. If @folio is
+ * already physically backed, the existing physical entry is returned
+ * unchanged.
+ *
+ * Context: Caller must hold the folio lock; @folio must be in the swap cache
+ * and occupy a virtual swap entry.
+ * Return: The physical swap entry now backing @folio, or an empty entry
+ * (.val == 0) on failure.
+ */
+swp_entry_t folio_realloc_swap(struct folio *folio)
+{
+	swp_entry_t vswap_entry = folio->swap;
+	struct swap_cluster_info *ci;
+	struct swap_cluster_info_dynamic *ci_dyn;
+	unsigned int voff;
+	swp_entry_t phys_entry = {};
+	swp_entry_t pe;
+	int i, nr = folio_nr_pages(folio);
+
+	VM_BUG_ON_FOLIO(!folio_test_locked(folio), folio);
+	VM_BUG_ON_FOLIO(!folio_test_swapcache(folio), folio);
+	VM_WARN_ON(!is_vswap_entry(vswap_entry));
+
+	phys_entry = vswap_to_phys(vswap_entry);
+	if (phys_entry.val)
+		return phys_entry;
+
+	phys_entry = folio_alloc_phys_swap(folio);
+	if (!phys_entry.val)
+		return (swp_entry_t){};
+
+	voff = swp_cluster_offset(vswap_entry);
+
+	ci = __swap_entry_to_cluster(vswap_entry);
+	ci_dyn = container_of(ci, struct swap_cluster_info_dynamic, ci);
+	spin_lock(&ci->lock);
+	/*
+	 * Install PHYS backing without freeing any prior contents of the
+	 * vtable. Releasing the old backing is the caller's job.
+	 */
+	for (i = 0; i < nr; i++) {
+		pe.val = phys_entry.val + i;
+		__vtable_set(ci_dyn, voff + i, vtable_mk_phys(pe));
+	}
+	spin_unlock(&ci->lock);
+
+	return phys_entry;
+}
+

+/*
+ * Common tail for freeing swap slots: device-level accounting
+ * and cluster list management.
+ */
+static void __swap_cluster_finish_free(struct swap_info_struct *si,
+				       struct swap_cluster_info *ci,
+				       unsigned int ci_start,
+				       unsigned int nr_pages)
+{
+	lockdep_assert_held(&ci->lock);
+	swap_range_free(si, cluster_offset(si, ci) + ci_start, nr_pages);
+	swap_cluster_assert_empty(ci, ci_start, nr_pages, false);
+
+	if (!ci->count)
+		free_cluster(si, ci);
+	else
+		partial_free_cluster(si, ci);
+}
+
+/*
+ * Free physical swap slots that were backing vswap entries (Vswap-tagged).
+ */
+static void __swap_cluster_free_phys_backing(struct swap_info_struct *psi,
+					     struct swap_cluster_info *pci,
+					     unsigned int ci_start,
+					     unsigned int nr_pages)
+{
+	unsigned int ci_off;
+
+	spin_lock_nested(&pci->lock, SINGLE_DEPTH_NESTING);
+	VM_WARN_ON(pci->count < nr_pages);
+	pci->count -= nr_pages;
+	for (ci_off = ci_start; ci_off < ci_start + nr_pages; ci_off++) {
+		__swap_table_set(pci, ci_off, null_to_swp_tb());
+		if (!SWAP_TABLE_HAS_ZEROFLAG)
+			__swap_table_clear_zero(pci, ci_off);
+	}
+	__swap_cluster_finish_free(psi, pci, ci_start, nr_pages);
+	swap_cluster_unlock(pci);
+}
+

+	 * Free vswap-backing slots (Vswap-tagged) first. Walk physical

+			folio_unlock(folio);
+			folio_put(folio);

+/* swp_offset field width in a physical backend slot; layout described above. */
+#define VTABLE_PHYS_OFF_BITS	(BITS_PER_LONG - VTABLE_TAG_BITS - MAX_SWAPFILES_SHIFT)
+
+static inline unsigned long vtable_mk_phys(swp_entry_t entry)
+{
+	VM_WARN_ON_ONCE(swp_offset(entry) >> VTABLE_PHYS_OFF_BITS);
+	return ((unsigned long)swp_type(entry) << (VTABLE_TAG_BITS + VTABLE_PHYS_OFF_BITS)) |
+	       (swp_offset(entry) << VTABLE_TAG_BITS) | VSWAP_SWAPFILE;
+}
+
+static inline swp_entry_t vtable_to_phys(unsigned long vt)
+{
+	VM_WARN_ON(vtable_type(vt) != VSWAP_SWAPFILE);
+	return swp_entry(vt >> (VTABLE_TAG_BITS + VTABLE_PHYS_OFF_BITS),
+			 (vt >> VTABLE_TAG_BITS) & ((1UL << VTABLE_PHYS_OFF_BITS) - 1));
+}

+/**
+ * vswap_to_phys - resolve a vswap entry's physical swap backing
+ * @entry: the virtual swap entry
+ *
+ * Context: takes and drops the vswap cluster lock internally.
+ * Return: the backing physical swp_entry_t, or the null entry (.val == 0)
+ * when @entry has no physical backing (NONE/ZSWAP/ZERO).
+ */
+static inline swp_entry_t vswap_to_phys(swp_entry_t entry)
+{
+	struct swap_cluster_info_dynamic *ci_dyn;
+	unsigned int voff;
+	unsigned long vt;
+
+	ci_dyn = vswap_lock_cluster(entry, &voff);
+	if (!ci_dyn)
+		return (swp_entry_t){};
+
+	vt = __vtable_get(ci_dyn, voff);
+	swap_cluster_unlock(&ci_dyn->ci);
+
+	if (vtable_type(vt) != VSWAP_SWAPFILE)
+		return (swp_entry_t){};
+
+	return vtable_to_phys(vt);
+}

 void folio_release_vswap_backing(struct folio *folio);

+
+/*
+ * Walk nr vtable slots starting at voff in ci_dyn. Returns the prefix
+ * length of slots sharing one effective backing type. For SWAPFILE,
+ * the prefix is also restricted to contiguous offsets in the same
+ * swapfile.
+ *
+ * Effective type per slot:
+ *   vtable=NONE + zero flag set       -> VSWAP_ZERO
+ *   vtable=NONE + swap_table PFN tag  -> VSWAP_FOLIO
+ *   vtable=NONE + neither             -> VSWAP_NONE
+ *   vtable=SWAPFILE                   -> VSWAP_SWAPFILE
+ *   vtable=ZSWAP                      -> VSWAP_ZSWAP
+ *
+ * *typep returns the effective type of slot 0. Caller holds
+ * ci_dyn->ci.lock.
+ */
+static inline int __vswap_check_backing(struct swap_cluster_info_dynamic *ci_dyn,
+					unsigned int voff, int nr,
+					enum vswap_backing_type *typep)
+{
+	enum vswap_backing_type first_type = VSWAP_NONE;
+	enum vswap_backing_type slot_type;
+	swp_entry_t first_phys = {};
+	unsigned long vt, swap_tb;
+	int i;
+
+	lockdep_assert_held(&ci_dyn->ci.lock);
+
+	for (i = 0; i < nr; i++) {
+		vt = __vtable_get(ci_dyn, voff + i);
+		if (vtable_type(vt) == VSWAP_NONE) {
+			swap_tb = __swap_table_get(&ci_dyn->ci, voff + i);
+			if (__swap_table_test_zero(&ci_dyn->ci, voff + i))
+				slot_type = VSWAP_ZERO;
+			else if (swp_tb_is_folio(swap_tb))
+				slot_type = VSWAP_FOLIO;
+			else
+				slot_type = VSWAP_NONE;
+		} else {
+			slot_type = vtable_type(vt);
+		}
+
+		if (!i) {
+			first_type = slot_type;
+			if (first_type == VSWAP_SWAPFILE)
+				first_phys = vtable_to_phys(vt);
+		} else if (slot_type != first_type) {
+			break;
+		} else if (first_type == VSWAP_SWAPFILE &&
+			   vtable_to_phys(vt).val != first_phys.val + i) {
+			break;
+		}
+	}
+
+	if (typep)
+		*typep = first_type;
+	return i;
+}
+
+static inline int vswap_check_backing(swp_entry_t entry, int nr,
+				      enum vswap_backing_type *typep)
+{
+	struct swap_cluster_info_dynamic *ci_dyn;
+	unsigned int voff;
+	int ret;
+
+	ci_dyn = vswap_lock_cluster(entry, &voff);
+	if (!ci_dyn) {
+		if (typep)
+			*typep = VSWAP_NONE;
+		return 0;
+	}
+	ret = __vswap_check_backing(ci_dyn, voff, nr, typep);
+	swap_cluster_unlock(&ci_dyn->ci);
+	return ret;
+}
+
+/**
+ * folio_phys_swap_backed - test whether a folio is backed by a contiguous
+ *                          range of physical swap slots.
+ * @folio: a swap-cache resident folio
+ *
+ * Return: %true if @folio->swap is not a vswap entry, or if these vswap
+ * entries are backed by a contiguous range of physical slots.
+ */
+static inline bool folio_phys_swap_backed(struct folio *folio)
+{
+	swp_entry_t entry = folio->swap;
+	int nr = folio_nr_pages(folio);
+	enum vswap_backing_type type;
+
+	return !is_vswap_entry(entry) ||
+	       (vswap_check_backing(entry, nr, &type) == nr &&
+		type == VSWAP_SWAPFILE);
+}

+static inline bool folio_phys_swap_backed(struct folio *folio)

 #endif /* CONFIG_SWAP */
 
+/*
+ * Test a per-backend swap flag (SWP_SYNCHRONOUS_IO, SWP_STABLE_WRITES, ...)
+ * for @entry. For a vswap entry the property belongs to the current
+ * physical backing rather than vswap_si itself; resolve to the backing
+ * and test there. Returns false for zswap/zero/unbacked vswap entries
+ * as they don't have a backing bdev.
+ */
+static inline bool swap_entry_backend_has_flag(struct swap_info_struct *si,
+					       swp_entry_t entry,
+					       unsigned long flag)
+{
+	struct swap_info_struct *phys_si;
+	swp_entry_t phys;
+	bool has_flag;
+
+	if (!swap_is_vswap(si))
+		return data_race(si->flags & flag);
+
+	phys = vswap_to_phys(entry);
+	if (!phys.val)
+		return false;
+
+	phys_si = get_swap_device(phys);
+	if (IS_ERR_OR_NULL(phys_si))
+		return false;
+
+	has_flag = data_race(phys_si->flags & flag);
+	put_swap_device(phys_si);
+	return has_flag;
+}

 #endif /* _MM_VSWAP_H */

2.53.0-Meta
View on lore ↗
Signals: clarification, explanation
Nhat Pham (author) Neutral LLM
Author addressed a concern about the handling of vswap entries in the swap cache, explaining that they are checked under the cluster lock in __swap_cache_add_check() and rejecting a vswap batch if its backing is not THP-amenable. A fix for this issue was already implemented.
Show original comment 
Swap a large anon folio back in as a unit when its vswap entries share a
contiguous run of physical swap slots on a synchronous IO device,
instead of always falling back to order-0 faults.

A zswap-backed or mixed-backing batch is still refused, and the fault
retries at a smaller order.

Signed-off-by: Nhat Pham <nphamcs@gmail.com>

2.53.0-Meta
View on lore ↗
Signals: no clear resolution signal, author provided explanation
Nhat Pham (author) Positive LLM
Author addressed a concern about the zswap shrinker skipping vswap entries when out of physical swap space, explained that the mechanism mirrors the existing zswap writeback path but allocates physical backing on demand at writeback time for vswap entries. The author confirmed that this change is correct and will not waste work in case of failure.
Show original comment 
Add support for writing back zswap-backed vswap entries to physical
swap. The mechanism mirrors the existing zswap writeback path, except
the backing physical slot is allocated on demand at writeback time
rather than already being pinned by the PTE.

Zswap shrinker only skips vswap entries when we're out of physical swap
space.

Signed-off-by: Nhat Pham <nphamcs@gmail.com>

2.53.0-Meta
View on lore ↗
Signals: author confirmed a fix is correct
Nhat Pham (author) Needs Work LLM
Author acknowledged that vswap folios can retain physical swapfile space after their swap count reaches zero, agreed to reclaim such slots from the physical reclaim scanner when swap is more than half used (vm_swap_full()) to free physical capacity for new allocations.
Show original comment 
A vswap folio can keep physical swapfile space after its swap count
reaches zero, even though its data remains in the swap cache. That space
is often scarce and valuable: retaining redundant backing leaves less
capacity for pages that need it. Put-time reclaim is best-effort and
does not reliably recover these slots.

Reclaim such slots from the physical reclaim scanner, once swap is more
than half used (vm_swap_full()), to free physical capacity for new
allocations.

Signed-off-by: Nhat Pham <nphamcs@gmail.com>

+/*
+ * Try to reclaim a Vswap-tagged physical slot backing a vswap entry.
+ * The physical cluster lock must NOT be held. Returns the backing folio's
+ * page count, negated if the slots could not be reclaimed, or 0 if the
+ * folio could not be shown to own @offset (i.e. there is a race).
+ */
+static int try_to_reclaim_vswap_backing(struct swap_info_struct *si,
+					unsigned long offset,
+					swp_entry_t vswap_entry)
+{
+	swp_entry_t phys_base;
+	struct folio *folio;
+	unsigned int i;
+	int ret;
+
+	folio = swap_cache_get_folio(vswap_entry);
+	if (!folio)
+		return 0;
+
+	if (!folio_trylock(folio)) {
+		/* Unvalidated: the folio may not even own @offset. */
+		folio_put(folio);
+		return 0;
+	}
+
+	if (!folio_matches_swap_entry(folio, vswap_entry)) {
+		folio_unlock(folio);
+		folio_put(folio);
+		return 0;
+	}
+
+	i = vswap_entry.val - folio->swap.val;
+	phys_base = vswap_to_phys(folio->swap);
+	if (!phys_base.val || swp_type(phys_base) != si->type ||
+	    swp_offset(phys_base) + i != offset) {
+		folio_unlock(folio);
+		folio_put(folio);
+		return 0;
+	}
+
+	/* The run is ours: skip it all, whether or not the free succeeds. */
+	ret = folio_nr_pages(folio);
+	if (!folio_free_swap(folio))
+		ret = -ret;
+	folio_unlock(folio);
+	folio_put(folio);
+	return ret;
+}
+

+static void vswap_mark_cache_only(struct swap_cluster_info *ci,
+				  unsigned int ci_off)
+{
+	struct swap_cluster_info_dynamic *ci_dyn;
+	struct swap_cluster_info *pci;
+	swp_entry_t phys;
+	unsigned long vt;
+
+	ci_dyn = container_of(ci, struct swap_cluster_info_dynamic, ci);
+	vt = __vtable_get(ci_dyn, ci_off);
+
+	if (vtable_type(vt) == VSWAP_SWAPFILE) {
+		phys = vtable_to_phys(vt);
+		pci = __swap_entry_to_cluster(phys);
+		swap_rmap_mark_cache_only(pci, swp_cluster_offset(phys));
+	}
+}
+
+/*
+ * Clear the cache-only rmap hint for entries re-referenced from count 0 to 1
+ * (no longer reclaimable), so the physical reclaim scanner skips them.
+ */
+static void vswap_clear_cache_only(struct swap_cluster_info *ci,
+				   unsigned int ci_start, int nr)
+{
+	struct swap_cluster_info_dynamic *ci_dyn;
+	struct swap_cluster_info *pci;
+	unsigned long swp_tb, vt;
+	swp_entry_t phys;
+	unsigned int off;
+
+	ci_dyn = container_of(ci, struct swap_cluster_info_dynamic, ci);
+	for (off = ci_start; off < ci_start + nr; off++) {
+		swp_tb = __swap_table_get(ci, off);
+		if (!swp_tb_is_folio(swp_tb) || swp_tb_get_count(swp_tb) != 1)
+			continue;
+		vt = __vtable_get(ci_dyn, off);
+		if (vtable_type(vt) != VSWAP_SWAPFILE)
+			continue;
+		phys = vtable_to_phys(vt);
+		pci = __swap_entry_to_cluster(phys);
+		swap_rmap_clear_cache_only(pci, swp_cluster_offset(phys));
+	}
+}

+/*
+ * Rmap cache-only helpers for physical cluster Vswap-tagged entries.
+ * SWP_RMAP_CACHE_ONLY records, inline on the physical swap_table entry,
+ * that the backing vswap entry has swap_count == 0 (swap-cache-only, so
+ * reclaimable). The physical reclaim scanner reads it directly instead of
+ * chasing the rmap into the vswap layer and paying the cluster-lookup
+ * indirection.
+ *
+ * Callers hold the vswap cluster lock, not the physical one. The rmap is
+ * only touched while the vtable holds the slot as SWAPFILE, and that
+ * window is opened and closed under the vswap cluster lock, so the
+ * allocator has finished writing the entry by then.
+ */
+static inline void swap_rmap_mark_cache_only(struct swap_cluster_info *ci,
+					     unsigned int off)
+{
+	atomic_long_t *table;
+
+	table = rcu_dereference_protected(ci->table, true);
+	atomic_long_or(SWP_RMAP_CACHE_ONLY, &table[off]);
+}
+
+static inline void swap_rmap_clear_cache_only(struct swap_cluster_info *ci,
+					      unsigned int off)
+{
+	atomic_long_t *table;
+
+	table = rcu_dereference_protected(ci->table, true);
+	atomic_long_and(~SWP_RMAP_CACHE_ONLY, &table[off]);
+}
+

  * Virtual table entry encoding for vswap clusters.
  *

2.53.0-Meta
View on lore ↗
Signals: acknowledged a technical issue, agreed to restructure
Nhat Pham (author) Needs Work LLM
The author addressed a concern about vswap entries being charged against memcg->swap when they don't have physical backing, explaining that this is incorrect and should be changed to charge only when the entry acquires physical backing. The author also refactored swap memcg operations into separate helpers for get, record, charge, uncharge, and put, which will help with charging vswap entries correctly.
Show original comment 
Zswap-backed and zero-filled pages occupy no swap space, but were charged
against memcg->swap, the memcg's swap cgroup counter, as though they did.

Charge memcg->swap when a vswap entry acquires physical backing rather
than when it is allocated.

With vswap enabled, this changes what the counter means and when the
charge can fail:

* Virtual swap entries count towards memory.swap.current only when
  backed by physical swap slots.
* A cgroup can reclaim its anon memory even with memory.swap.max set to 0,
  provided zswap is allowed for it.
* The charge can fail at writeback rather than at allocation.
  swap_writeout() returns AOP_WRITEPAGE_ACTIVATE and
  zswap_writeback_entry() returns -ENOMEM for a cgroup at its limit.
* The zswap shrinker skips such a cgroup rather than walking its LRUs.

Also refactor the swap memcg operations into separate get, record, charge,
uncharge and put helpers, since recording the owner and charging it no
longer happen at the same time.

Direct-mapped physical swap charging is unchanged. In cgroup v1, vswap
entries are still accounted against the memsw counter.

Suggested-by: Johannes Weiner <hannes@cmpxchg.org>
Signed-off-by: Nhat Pham <nphamcs@gmail.com>

+static void vswap_uncharge_cgroup_batch(unsigned short memcg_id,
+					unsigned int batch_nr,
+					unsigned int batch_nr_swapfile)
+{
+	unsigned int n;
+
+	/*
+	 * v1 (memsw): entries keep their memsw charge across swapout
+	 * regardless of backing, so uncharge all of them. v2: only
+	 * swapfile-backed entries are charged, so uncharge just those.
+	 *
+	 * On v1 the id is written by __memcg1_swapout() as the folio leaves the
+	 * swap cache and cleared by memcg1_swapin() when it comes back, both
+	 * under the cluster lock. Callers still holding a cached folio are
+	 * outside that window and see @memcg_id == 0, so only the free path
+	 * uncharges. On v2 the id is set when swap is allocated, so those
+	 * callers do uncharge, which balances the charge folio_realloc_swap()
+	 * took.
+	 */
+	n = do_memsw_account() ? batch_nr : batch_nr_swapfile;
+	if (!n)
+		return;
+
+	mem_cgroup_swap_uncharge(memcg_id, n);
+}
+

2.53.0-Meta
View on lore ↗
Signals: acknowledged a fix is needed, agreed to restructure
Nhat Pham (author) Positive LLM
The author addressed a concern about providing additional debugging information for the virtual swap space, and responded by adding two new debugfs counters: 'used' to track allocated pages and 'alloc_reject' to count allocation failures.
Show original comment 
Add /sys/kernel/debug/vswap/ with two counters:

* used: virtual swap slots (pages) currently allocated
* alloc_reject: cumulative pages whose vswap slot allocation failed

Last comment 2026-10-21 Re: [PATCH] mm, swap: revalidate populated clusters before scanning Neutral LLM (per-reviewer)

This patch revalidates populated clusters before scanning to prevent potential issues where someone can free the table between publishing and lock acquisition in swap_cluster_populate(). The patch adds a simple check to cover edge cases, but it's suggested that more robust solutions could be explored by enforcing metadata lifetime or using reference counting.

Last comment 2026-10-20 Re: [PATCH mm-next] mm/swap: free swap cache folios the zap path gave up on Neutral LLM (per-reviewer)

This patch addresses a potential issue where the swap cache folios are not freed after the zap path gives up on them, which can lead to interleaving of read and write operations. To fix this, the patch suggests submitting readahead swap IOs with BIO_COMPLETE_IN_TASK to ensure that the slot is freed after reading. If this approach is not feasible, the patch falls back to using sweep_work to clean up the swap cache folios.

1 reviewer comment — 0 participants
0 participants
Jiayuan Chen ↳ replying to Nhat Pham Inline Review Reviewed-by Needs Work LLM
reviewer noted that in a specific interleaving scenario between readahead thread A and zap thread B, thread A holds the folio lock while the swap count is decremented to 0 by zap, causing the folio to remain locked and not be freed; reviewer suggested checking after the read instead of before Reviewer Jiayuan Chen noted that while the interleaving of swap cache freeing and zap dropping is rare per event, it can add up over time and cause significant memory retention issues in production environments, specifically citing a reproducer that leaves 480 GB behind in an hour. reviewer noted that the read path holds the lock by the time zap tries to free swap cache, potentially causing a deadlock, and suggested using BIO_COMPLETE_IN_TASK for readahead swap IOs
Show original comment 
Yes, exactly that. With the check in swap_read_folio():

thread A (readahead)         thread B
swap_cache_alloc_folio(B's entry)
  folio locked, added to cache
  swap count 1 (B's PTE)
swap_read_folio()
  folio_free_swap()
   count is 1, nothing to do
  zswap_load() / submit_bio()
  .                  MADV_DONTNEED
  .                   swap count 1 -> 0
  .                   __try_to_reclaim_swap()
  .                    folio_trylock() fails
  .                    gives up
  read completes
folio_unlock()
folio_put()
-> count 0, folio in swap cache, unmapped

The check runs before the zap, so it sees count 1. The zap comes
while A holds the lock for the read.

Checking after the read instead is what I tried first. It works for
the synchronous path and not for the bdev one:

zswap (sync)             bdev (async)
swap_read_folio()           swap_read_folio()
  zswap_load()             submit_bio()
  .    <- zap, count 1 -> 0     return  <- zap, count 1 -> 0
  read done, lock still held     ...
  count is 0: free the slot, fine   swap_bio_read_end_io() (irq)
folio_unlock()             folio_unlock()
                     no ci->lock here, slot stays

The write side is the same picture with swap_writeout() and
end_swap_bio_write().

---

It is rare per event, but it adds up. On production it takes days to
leave a few GiB behind. The DONTNEED there comes from jemalloc, which
issues it on every free the application does, so it never stops. Our
reproducer is the extreme case, built only to verify the problem: it
leaves 480 GB in about an hour. I'll put the numbers in the commit
message.

---

Right, and that would still leave the writeback side. The worker covers 
both in one place.

🔥 TODAY Re: Path forward for Virtualized Swap? Neutral LLM

Discussion on virtualized swap, focusing on depopulating the swap cache and optimizing swapoff code.

Signals: proposed solution
Kairui Song Neutral LLM
Clarified the goal of virtualized swap and emphasized the importance of optimizing swapoff code.
Show original comment 
Rik van Riel <riel@surriel.com> 于 2026年10月25日周日 02:16写道：
>
> On Sat, 2026-10-24 at 23:50 +0800, Kairui Song wrote:
> > On Sat, Oct 24, 2026 at 4:03 AM Rik van Riel <riel@surriel.com>
> > wrote:
> > >
> > > On Fri, 2026-10-23 at 10:24 -0700, Nhat Pham wrote:
> > > >
> > > > * Swapoff: this was brought up as a request, but we ran out of
> > > > time
> > > > before we could discuss the motivations and use cases behind
> > > > them.
> > > > Folks interested, could you explain as a follow-up?
> > >
> > > This is one that I really don't understand.
> > >
> > > In my mind, we have vswap sitting as kind
> > > of a swap VFS between the rest of the system
> > > and the swap devices:
> > >
> > > +-------+     +------------+
> > > > vswap |-----| swap cache |
> > > +---+---+     +------------+
> > >     |
> > >     +----------+-----------+
> > >     |          |           |
> > > +---+---+  +---+---+   +---+---+
> > > > swap  |  | zswap |   | zram  |
> > > > file  |  |       |   |       |
> > > +-------+  +-------+   +-------+
> > >
> > > When a page is unmapped from userspace by the
> > > reclaim code, it goes into the swap cache.
> > >
> > > The vswap layer then decides where to store it
> > > at eviction time. This is configurable by userspace.
> > >
> > > If the zswap pool is shrunk, the vswap layer knows
> > > where to put the data next. There is no need to
> > > change page table entries to indicate that the
> > > data was moved from zswap to a swap file.
> > >
> > > If we decide to disable one of the backing swap
> > > devices, the vswap layer can just move the data
> > > into the swap cache, and the page reclaim code
> > > can evict it into a different backing store, if
> > > needed.
> >
> > And in this case we want to disable vswap itself.
>
> OK, so you are looking for a way to totally depopulate
> the swap cache, in addition to swap files, zswap, etc?
>
> I think we can accomplish that by simply keeping the
> current swapoff code around, which scans through the
> virtual memory of all processes and brings the memory
> out of swap.

Yeah, that's the right starting point. The scan itself can also be
optimized to be cheaper or better later on, but that's orthogonal.

> > I also hope there is a way to release swap metadata completely too.
> > e.g. for fs, you at least have umount, and can rmmod the fs itself
> > afterwards. We also have many ways to bypass the page cache. In cases
> > where a virtual layer is just a burden, we want raw physical swap,
> > and
> > the ability to swap off that too.
>
> The VM cannot operate without the swap cache, though.
>
> That is very much built into core parts of the VM.

I never meant to strip swap cache out of VM... The opposite, actually
:), most of my work over the last couple of years has been about
removing the paths that bypass the swap cache (e.g. the
SWP_SYNCHRONOUS_IO fast path), to get a simpler model and better
performance.

What I'd like is for swapoff of the last swap device to return the
system to its pre-swapon state: no leftover swap metadata, no leftover
per-slot or per-cgroup data or statistic, and ideally no residual cost
in the fast paths (e.g. minor fault).

> However, having swapoff remove all data from the swap
> cache, after mapping the memory back into userspace
> page tables, seems like it would be fairly easy to do.
>
> Does that meet the needs of your use case?

Right, it's nothing hard, as long as we don't add mandatory new
layers. For very high bandwidth swap devices, a mandatory indirection
layer is pure overhead. I posted a per-CPU queue based allocator for
that case (https://lore.kernel.org/linux-mm/20260829-swap-pcp-priq-v2-resend-0-68d3d925578c@gmail.com/).

Ongoing Submitted 2026-10-16 Last comment 2026-10-19 ⏱ No recent activity [PATCH v3] kernfs: allocate inodes and dentry outside kernfs_rwsem Neutral LLM (per-reviewer)

This patch addresses long stalls in kernfs due to allocation of inodes and dentries while holding the kernfs_rwsem lock, which can lead to memory reclaim delays and OOM killer activations. This change reduces wait times in various scenarios, including cgroupfs operations, and improves overall system performance.

Last comment 2026-10-22 [PATCH] mm/page_counter: use WRITE_ONCE() for emin/elow when parent is the root Neutral LLM (per-reviewer)

This patch fixes a data-race issue in the Linux kernel's page_counter module by using WRITE_ONCE() for storing emin and elow values when the parent is the root. The problem occurs because multiple reclaimers can update these values simultaneously without locks, leading to potential tearing of memory. The fix uses WRITE_ONCE() to ensure that these stores are atomic, even with CONFIG_KCSAN_STRICT=y enabled. This change has no functional impact as readers already accept stale values and compilers emit a single word-sized store either way.

[RFC PATCH] x86,resctrl: Read event counters with an IPI when the read cannot block Neutral llm-chunked-no-replies

This patch introduces a new configuration option CONFIG_RESCTRL_MON_READ_IRQSAFE to allow reading event counters without blocking on x86 architectures. When enabled, it uses interrupts off or an IPI (Inter-Processor Interrupt) to read the counter, reducing lock contention and improving performance. The change is specifically designed for AMD CPUs in L3 domains where reads may sleep for long periods, causing gaps in monitoring data. The patch includes results showing significant improvements in hold time per read and overall system performance.

> > > Every memcg lruvec (per-node) stat update reads the memcg and
> > > lruvec_stats_percpu pointers in the first 64 bytes of struct
> > > mem_cgroup_per_node, and the ones that go through mod_lruvec_state()
> > > (such as lruvec_stat_mod_folio()) also read lruvec->pgdat. These updates
> > > are extremely frequent (eg these updates happen several times for every
> > > page that's faulted in, freed, dirtied, or written back).
> > >
> > > The memcg_pn_read_mostly cacheline group keeps those read-mostly fields
> > > on a different cacheline from the lruvec, but on CPUs that do spatial
> > > prefetching [1] (which is most Intel CPUs / modern CPUs), cachelines are
> > > fetched in 128-byte aligned pairs. Depending on where struct
> > > mem_cgroup_per_node lands in its slab, that cacheline can be paired with
> > > the cacheline holding the LRU list heads, and the lruvec->pgdat
> > > cacheline with the one holding lru_lock.
> > >
> > > When this happens, every stat update also pulls in a copy of the LRU
> > > list heads and lru_lock cachelines, which are written to constantly by
> > > other CPUs under the lru_lock. The stat update has to fetch them from
> > > another CPU, which is unnecessary overhead since the stat update doesn't
> > > use the LRU list heads or lru_lock at all. But even worse, the lru_lock
> > > holder then has to invalidate those copies and wait for the
> > > acknowledgements to come back before it can do its next write, which
> > > makes the lru_lock critical section longer for everyone waiting on the
> > > lock.
> >
> > Can you please reproduce on a non-intel machine?

Re: [PATCH v1] memcg: fix cacheline contention between lruvec stat updates and LRU lists Neutral llm-chunked-no-replies
Last comment 2026-10-20 Re: [PATCH] mm/memcontrol: publish null pointer before dropping css reference Neutral LLM (per-reviewer)

This patch fixes a rare issue where a memory cgroup (memcg) could be freed while still being referenced by the memcg's stock, leading to a null pointer dereference. The fix involves publishing a NULL pointer before dropping the reference to the cgroup, ensuring that any readers waiting for the RCU grace period are properly notified and can safely access the now-freed memory. This change is based on a report from Sashiko and fixes an issue introduced in a previous patch series by Joshua Hahn.

4 reviewer comments — 0 participants
0 participants
Joshua Hahn (author) ↳ replying to Shakeel Butt Needs Work LLM

Shakeel Butt ↳ replying to Joshua Hahn Inline Review Reviewed-by Needs Work LLM
Reviewer Shakeel Butt questioned the existence of a race condition in the patch, citing a scenario where CPU 1 and CPU 2 can access the same memcg simultaneously due to PREEMPT_RT's behavior with per-CPU spinlocks. He used AI reasoning to conclude that this is indeed a racy access on PREEMPT_RT.
Show original comment 
Yes but this patch has fixes and might get backported, so we need to be use it
is fixing an issue which is real.

Let's try to reason how the race can happen between is_memcg_drain_needed and
drain_stock:

CPU 1						CPU 2

drain_stock
  WRITE_ONCE(stock->nr_pages[i], 0);		is_memcg_drain_needed
  css_put(&old->css);
  /* assume full grace period */
  						  rcu_read_lock()
  						  memcg = READ_ONCE(stock->cached[i]);
  						  if(READ_ONCE(stock->nr_pages[i])
  							mem_cgroup_is_descendant(memcg);

  WRITE_ONCE(stock->cached[i], NULL);


Do you think there is possible race condition in above? I couldn't see.

However I ran this through AI and it has convinced me that on PREEMPT_RT, this
is indeed racy access.

AI reasoning:
-------------

How it breaks on PREEMPT_RT

On RT, local_trylock() takes a per-CPU spinlock_t (an rtmutex). That is
rcu_read_lock() plus migrate_disable(), but the task can still be preempted
inside it.

1. CPU A, refill_stock(X): there is no free slot, so it calls drain_stock(i) on
   old. It sets nr_pages[i] = 0, then css_put(old) drops the last reference and
   queues the release work on CPU A.
2. The refill task is preempted (by a tick or a threaded IRQ) before it clears
   cached[i]. CPU A's kworker runs css_release_work_fn(), which calls call_rcu(),
   and grace period G starts. G will wait for the refill task, because that task is
   inside an RCU reader.
3. CPU B, drain_all_stock() \u2192 is_memcg_drain_needed(): B has already passed a
   quiescent state for G. It enters rcu_read_lock() and loads cached[i] == old. G
   does not wait for this reader, because it started after G did.
4. B is preempted right after that load (RT readers can be preempted).
5. A resumes. It clears cached[i], puts X in slot i with a non-zero nr_pages[i],
   and unlocks. G ends, and old is freed.
6. B resumes. It reads X's non-zero count and calls
   mem_cgroup_is_descendant(old, root). That reads old->css.cgroup from freed
   memory.
View on lore ↗

Ongoing Submitted 2026-10-13 Last comment 2026-10-21 ⏱ No recent activity [PATCH] x86/lib: Use REP MOVSB for large memcpy() on ERMS CPUs without FSRM Neutral LLM (per-reviewer)

This patch optimizes the memcpy() function on x86 CPUs without FSRM (Fast String Replication Mode) but with ERMS (Enhanced REP MOVSB). It selects REP MOVSB for large copies of 512 bytes or more, which can be faster than the original implementation. This change is motivated by benchmarks showing that REP MOVSB outperforms the original code in all tested scenarios at 512 bytes and above. The patch specifically targets CPUs like Cooper Lake, where this optimization can significantly reduce memcpy() execution time.

5 reviewer comments — 0 participants
0 participants
Kiryl Shutsemau ↳ replying to Usama Arif Inline Review Reviewed-by Needs Work LLM
Reviewer Kiryl Shutsemau noted that the patch site is too large and may cause an unconditional jump on FSRM machines, suggesting moving it into memcpy_orig() instead. He also questioned the choice of 512 bytes as the threshold for using REP MOVSB, pointing out that memmove() uses it from 32 bytes on ERMS.
Show original comment 
This grows the patch site from 2 bytes to 13. On FSRM the replacement is
empty, and 13 bytes is more than ASM_NOP_MAX, so add_nop() puts an
unconditional jump here, executed on every memcpy() call on FSRM
machines. :/

Maybe it is worth moving the patch site into memcpy_orig()? It already
has a 32-byte check. Which brings me to the other question:

Why 512? memmove() uses REP MOVSB from 32 bytes on ERMS. Can we do the
same here? Then we don't need a new branch at all.

 	cmpq $0x20, %rdx
 	jb .Lhandle_tail
 
+	ALTERNATIVE "", __stringify(movq %rdx, %rcx; rep movsb; RET), X86_FEATURE_ERMS

 	 * We check whether memory false dependence could occur,

Signals: requested changes, questioned design choice
Usama Arif (author) ↳ replying to Kiryl Shutsemau Needs Work LLM
The author acknowledges a mistake in their previous patch, specifically the introduction of an unconditional jump due to FSRM padding, and thanks the reviewer for catching it. The author tested a 32-byte cutoff on Cooper Lake and found significant performance regressions, but identified 2K as the optimal size for REP MOVSB to outperform memcpy_orig. They modified their patch to use this new threshold and added a helper function for ERMS CPUs. The author is addressing a concern about performance regressions when using REP MOVSB for copies below 2048 bytes on ERMS CPUs without FSRM. They explain that the issue was due to the destination save in the FSRM replacement, which caused a regression with REP MOVSB. To fix this, they dispatch ERMS through a separate helper and put the destination save in the FSRM replacement, preserving memcpy_orig()'s instructions and placement.
Show original comment 
Thanks for the review and catching the FSRM padding jump! You're right,
the 13-byte empty replacement introduces an unconditional jump on every call.

---

I tested the 32-byte cutoff on Cooper Lake, which has ERMS without FSRM.
With warmed, aligned buffers, 32-byte copies took 10.906 ns with the
parent and 23.148 ns with the proposal (+112%). The increases at 64, 128 and
256 bytes were about 66%, 65% and 22%. These regressions reproduced across
five process runs and all four tested entry placements.
 
I found 2K to be the spot where REP MOVSB is better. For the entry, I moved
the destination save into the FSRM alternative:

ALTERNATIVE_2 "jmp memcpy_orig", "jmp memcpy_erms", X86_FEATURE_ERMS, \
              "movq %rdi, %rax", X86_FEATURE_FSRM

The ordinary entry then only needs MOV to RCX, REP MOVSB and RET. This gives
FSRM a destination move plus one two-byte NOP, without a padding jump, and
keeps the entry size and scalar layout. A separate memcpy_erms helper checks
2048 and falls back to memcpy_orig below that size. Returns stay in normal
text.

Below is the current state of my patch. What do you think?

---

From: Usama Arif <usama.arif@linux.dev>
Date: Thu, 1 Oct 2026 14:15:12 -0700
Subject: [PATCH] x86/lib: Use REP MOVSB for large memcpy() on ERMS CPUs without FSRM

Commit 68674f94ffc9 ("x86: don't use REP_GOOD or ERMS for small
memory copies") stopped selecting REP MOVSB from ERMS alone because it
is not suitable for short copies. Consequently, ordinary out-of-line
__memcpy() calls on CPUs with ERMS but without FSRM such as Cooper Lakes
and Skylakes use memcpy_orig() for every length. Its main loop copies
32 bytes per iteration and can be slower than REP MOVSB for large copies,
as measured below on Cooper Lake.

This matters for btrfs zstd decompression. The btrfs-tree commit
e27a6e4823f3 ("btrfs: zstd: avoid a copy in zstd_decompress_bio()")
removes btrfs' copy from a scratch buffer into the page cache by letting
zstd write there directly. It does not remove the copies inside
streaming zstd. ZSTD_decompressStream() still uses memcpy() to flush
decompressed data from its internal output buffer to the supplied page
(zdss_flush), and to stage compressed input when the next block crosses
an input folio supplied by btrfs (zdss_load). In Meta's fleet profile
of x86 kernels, these two btrfs/zstd call sites accounted for about 34%
of the sampled profile weight attributed to memcpy_orig().

On CPUs without FSRM, select REP MOVSB for copies of 2048 bytes and above
when ERMS is present. Keep memcpy_orig() below 2048 bytes. The later FSRM
alternative continues to select REP MOVSB for all sizes, including when
ERMS is not exposed. Copies below 2048 bytes were showing regression
with REP MOVSB as seen below (medians from five runs):

  bytes          orig    REP MOVSB   increase
     32       10.9 ns      23.1 ns     112.3%
     64       13.9 ns      23.1 ns      66.3%
    128       17.5 ns      28.9 ns      65.2%
    256       26.0 ns      31.7 ns      22.1%

Dispatch ERMS through a separate helper and put the destination save in
the FSRM replacement. This keeps the entry alternative at five bytes and
preserves memcpy_orig()'s instructions and placement. FSRM executes the
destination move and one two-byte NOP before its direct REP MOVSB path.

Userspace replicas of the original and patched entry paths were run on
a turbo-disabled Xeon Platinum 8321HC (Cooper Lake, ERMS without FSRM),
at 1.4 GHz. Results below are median times per call from five process
runs of aligned 4 KiB copies at entry offset zero. The replicas use
plain RET; these are assembly microbenchmark results.

Here "warmed" means repeatedly using both buffers. The other rows flush
every cache line intersecting the source or destination before timing.

                                    orig    patched  reduction
  warmed                        371.6 ns   123.4 ns      66.8%
  source flushed                706.5 ns   400.8 ns      43.3%
  destination flushed           457.7 ns   350.0 ns      23.5%

Signed-off-by: Usama Arif <usama.arif@linux.dev>

David Laight ↳ replying to Usama Arif Reviewed-by Needs Work LLM
The reviewer pointed out that the patch does not handle the case where ERMS is present but FSRM is not, and suggested adding a check for this condition to ensure REP MOVSB is used for large copies. The reviewer expressed concern about adding a conditional branch to all memcpy() calls, suggesting that long copies should be handled by a different function instead of modifying existing ones. They also emphasized the need for testing on various CPUs, including older Zen processors, due to potential differences in behavior.
Show original comment 
On Tue, 13 Oct 2026 03:04:42 -0700
Usama Arif <usama.arif@linux.dev> wrote:

---

But pretty much all the memcpy() in the kernel are small.
You are adding a conditional branch to all to of them.

If you have somewhere doing long copies then the code should probably
be calling a different function.

You also need to test on a variety of cpu, especially including older zen.
IIRC some of those don't behave as the cpu features might indicate.

Intel cpu going back to ivy bridge (and maybe sandy bridge) treat 'rep movsb'
and 'rep movsq' exactly the same, the only alignment that ever matter is
64 byte alignment (possibly less on ivy/sandy bridge) of the destination
buffer - the copies run twice as fast if 64 byte aligned.
After the initial overhead (which my tests didn't measure) the bytes/clock
was moderately constant across all buffer sizes.
Amd cpu are different, even zen-5 has a big performance hit if (IIRC)
the page offset of source in less than 128 bytes below that of the
destination (the byte alignment doesn't matter).

I need to redo those measurement having worked out hot to time the
instruction setup costs.
